#6958. Jimmy Carter’s Family Farm Just Sold for $8.5 Million

The Georgia property will become part of a nearly 7,000-acre wildlife management area, with public access possible as early as 2027.

President Jimmy Carter’s family farmland near Plains, Georgia, was just sold to the Conservation Fund as part of an approximately $8.5 million acquisition that will protect nearly 2,500 acres of fields and woodland. Announced November 9, the deal sets the stage for the land’s eventual transfer to the state of Georgia, with public access potentially beginning as early as 2027.

The purchase encompasses approximately 1,600 acres owned by the Carter family, along with an adjoining tract. It does not include Carter’s boyhood home and farm, which are already preserved by the National Park Service as part of Jimmy Carter National Historical Park.

Instead, the newly acquired property is slated to join the nearby Lanahassee Creek Wildlife Management Area, expanding it to nearly 7,000 acres. The land provides habitat for gopher tortoises and other wildlife, while neighboring creeks support rare freshwater mussels. Plans call for the Georgia Department of Natural Resources to take ownership in 2028.

The acquisition also continues restoration work Carter initiated himself. At his request, the family preserved a stand of mature longleaf pine and replanted much of the surrounding acreage with the same native species. Once used to grow crops, including peanuts, the property became a retreat where he and Rosalynn spent time away from public life. The family acquired the land around the turn of the 20th century, according to the Conservation Fund.

“They liked to go and walk the land,” Blake Sullivan, who managed Carter’s farm and timberland for more than four decades, told The Washington Post. “For both of them, it was a place of rest and renewal.”

Carter continued visiting into his final years, arranging to be driven through the property when walking became difficult. Amy Carter, the president’s daughter, told The Post, “I think his very favorite place was to be in the town and on the land where he grew up, and where he had those very deep roots.”

#6956. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6954. Are You the Next Iconic Kitchen Designer?

The Monogram Iconic Kitchen Design Awards honor the visionaries redefining luxury kitchen design through exceptional creativity, craftsmanship, and innovation.

An extraordinary kitchen is defined by refined materials, exquisite details, and design that balances ease with sophistication. These spaces do more than serve a purpose; they elevate how we live. For designers who share this philosophy, the prestigious Monogram Iconic Kitchen Awards are built to recognize your vision.

Now through November 30, architects, interior designers, builders, and remodelers can submit their completed projects featuring Monogram appliances for the honor of industry recognition, features in ELLE Decor, House Beautiful, and Veranda, and cash prizes of up to $25,000.

“The Monogram Iconic Kitchen Awards are rooted in our belief that the kitchen is both a functional space and a deeply personal expression of how people live,” says Julie Burns, executive director of Monogram. “Through this program, we’re proud to celebrate the designers who push the boundaries of luxury kitchen design while creating spaces that are as livable as they are beautiful.”

The awards will recognize 12 finalists in four categories, with one winner in each. Categories include:

Burns notes that the Monogram Iconic Kitchen Design Awards identify work that “continues to raise the bar for luxury living while inspiring the future of kitchen design.” An esteemed panel of judges with a discerning eye for luxury design will evaluate the submissions based on five key elements: the thoughtful integration of Monogram in the design, design excellence, functionality and space planning, innovation, and originality, as well as the impact of the design statement and imagery.

Twelve finalists will be celebrated in February at the Kitchen & Bath Industry Show (KBIS) and winners will be announced during an exclusive celebration at the High Point Market in April. What makes a kitchen iconic? Exceptional design is more than what you see; it’s how a space makes you feel. An iconic kitchen represents the perfect harmony of aesthetic beauty and quiet purpose, crafted to leave a lasting impression.

#6952. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

#6947. Margot Robbie’s Side-Parted ‘Buttercup Bob’ Is Polished and Easy to Wear: How to Try It Now

The actor and producer proves how a simple styling switch can make all the difference.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There are few hair updates as simple, yet as impactful, as switching up your part. After several seasons dominated by center-parted lengths and deliberately undone styling, the side part is making a convincing return. The latest to make a case for its appeal? None other than Margot Robbie.

Attending Chanel’s Cruise 2026/2027 show in Australia, the actor and producer stepped out with a softly structured ‘buttercup bob,’ styled with a deep side part and flipped-out ends. The result was equal parts polished and quietly sophisticated. And while Robbie has recently been favoring the waterfall bob, this latest iteration feels notably sleeker, offering a more refined take on the trending cut.

“This bob is an effortless take on a classic jaw-length cut. It’s neat without feeling rigid, with soft ends and a clean shape that gives hair an expensive-looking finish,” Luke Hersheson, hairstylist and CEO of Hershesons previously told ELLE. In short, it trades lived-in texture for a more polished silhouette, with precise ends and subtle flicks upward that create a sharper finish.

Robbie’s latest style embodies the look perfectly, pairing a side part with softly sculpted ends for an updated take on the preppy bob. The part allows for a sweeping side bang, adding a touch of glamour to an otherwise classic cut.

When it comes to re-creating Robbie’s look, Talia Hurley, a stylist at Blue Tit London, recommends blow-drying the hair with a round brush for a glossy, classic finish. This will also help create added volume and the bend needed to achieve the look’s side-swept shape.

Luke Hersheson recommends finishing the look with a pea-sized amount of smoothing cream to add shine and tame flyaways without leaving the hair greasy, ensuring the style retains its polished, glossy finish.

#6945. Erdem Is on a Red Carpet Style Streak—Here’s Why

Celebrities from Kim Kardashian to Cynthia Erivo to Troye Sivan have pulled looks

At London Fashion Week this September, Erdem debuted a Spring 2027 collection that was instantly celebrated for its very on-brand tug-of-war between masculinity and femininity. Individual ensembles clashed crystal embellishment, tuxedo details, classic shirting, and unexpected flashes of skin. Critics praised it—and celebrity stylists began calling pieces in almost immediately.

Erdem has long had a slew of celebrity friends, supporters, and customers, but something about the reception this season feels different. In less than two months, runway looks have been worn by Kim Kardashian, Rosalía (twice), Cynthia Erivo, Sienna Miller, Troye Sivan, Honey Dijon, Cara Delevingne, Chappell Roan, Inde Navarette, Rosé, Keira Knightley, and Sarah Paulson. An unlikely collective perhaps, but each individual puts forth their own glorious interpretation of style and gender expression.

Erdem Moralıoğlu began this collection with the story of Barbette, the Texas-born trapeze artist whose performances, done in full drag, rose to prominence in Paris in the 1920s and ’30s. The clothes explore this sort of complex, androgynous dialogue between masculinity and femininity. The idea is perhaps best epitomized in a dress (worn by Knightley) that begins as a menswear-inspired gray knit before fading into a crystal-embellished sheer tulle skirt—and styled in the very Erdem manner with socks and black brogues. Or take Sivan’s ensemble: a black tuxedo jacket with satin and tulle draped over an ostrich feather pannier at the hip. Even the most markedly girlish looks, like Rosalía’s ’20s-inspired dress in blush pink silk, come with a certain restraint.

Moralıoğlu celebrated 20 years of Erdem this year and has built a ready-to-wear brand that is both distinctly recognizable and still able to evolve each season. Spring 2027 was not a radical departure for the designer, merely a looser approach to his typical decadence. (After all, Moralıoğlu has always paired his colorful jacquard dresses with simple black brogues.) It seems like a literal snapshot of consumers and their desires: clothes that combine elements of femininity and masculinity, utility and flourish. And it proves that two decades in, Moralıoğlu can continue to woo new audiences.

Ahead, we spoke with Moralıoğlu to gauge his reaction to all of this attention and reflect on what’s resonating most.

Well, the muse, as you know, was someone named “Barbette,” or Vander Clyde Broadway, who was originally from Austin, Texas, and then came to Paris and became this extraordinary muse and then kind of disappeared into obscurity. I love the idea of this idea of someone becoming something and that idea of self-actualization. But also, more importantly, Cocteau wrote a very interesting essay on Barbette and commissioned Man Ray to do this beautiful photo essay on him, as well. It was really about this idea of how relative masculinity and femininity is and about androgyny. So if I had to summarize it into one thing, there's a feeling of androgyny and flitting between masculine and feminine.

Yes, and I think what's interesting about Barbette as a character was that it was equally as feminine as it was masculine. In a way, they weren't trying to emulate becoming a woman, but they also weren't emulating a man. It's almost like the third sex, which is, I think, very interesting.

I definitely have noticed it, and it's definitely been noticed in the studio. All of us are like, "Wow, another one! It really seems to be happening.” But it's such a privilege to dress so many different people from so many different backgrounds and extraordinary, amazing, brilliant, talented people. I mean, it's wonderful. It's thrilling. I love it.

I mean, I can't pick just one. But I thought it was so interesting to see Troye in that piece, to see how that all worked and how it evolved. Interestingly enough, I had originally created trousers for that look, but didn’t use them for the show. So then the trousers came back out for that carpet. Otherwise, Troye was so loyal to how it was shown on the runway, even with the shirt styled exactly the same.

I still feel the same way. I think it's a privilege to be in a position where you dress extraordinarily talented people. That's a wonderful extension of your world that then becomes something else. It’s really interesting to see the collection take on another life. Otherwise, it just exists in the bubble of a show or an editorial.

The honest answer would be no, I don't think about it at all. I think about the collection and the character—her or them or whoever that person is. But when the show's coming together, you also really think about movement, how it's fitted, how something really moves on the body. And I wonder if that's something that's interesting in the context of it being outside of a runway show. I find Rosalia walking down the street in New York so fascinating. They're clothes that move and I really like that.

Well, I just left the other room where we’re working on the next one. But I like that psychology of turning the page. I find that really interesting. I love the idea of the blank, white page. I find it really exciting. But honest answer, no. [I don’t think about evolution]. Maybe what happens is that in time, you become a little bit looser. With that last show, there was something about that kind of world of Barbette. It was easy for us in the studio to really feel it, feel that masculine and feminine energy and to be very loose with it.

I usually tend to have a character. I always find it's like making a movie every six months. It's almost like a new script. I think it helps to take everyone collectively somewhere else. So you have to adjust together, but you have to have something to take everyone there with you.

Too difficult to say. In the context of these extraordinary women…Cynthia, I just think is amazing. Or Inde. And then Rosalía, Chappell, I'm like…

Let's think. Dietrich. [Marlene] Dietrich would be a good one. I love her.

Well, I think there’s something interesting about this idea of how you have a starting point and then you can end up somewhere else. How do you take the idea of something, and make it modern. How are you looking at something and finding its spirit? When you think about all of these questions of gender and expression, it's something that's always existed culturally. That's also a really beautiful thing.

#6944. Is This the Ultimate Aspen Anti-Chalet?

Designer Rafael de Cárdenas, known for creating arresting spaces for Skims, Nike, and a host of New York City restaurants, worked with longtime client Rachel Dorrell on a mountain retreat with otherworldly glam.

Above: The primary bathroom of a home in Aspen, Colorado, designed by Rafael de Cárdenas with architecture by SAOTA. Custom tub with Dornbracht fittings; ceramic vase by artist Kazunori Hamana; side table by Rafael Prieto.

“We’re having a crisis of taste,” architect and designer Rafael de Cárdenas is in the midst of saying. “There’s a sameness. It’s all a historical mishmash.” He’s at breakfast in Manhattan drinking a cinnamon latte (the restaurant did not have the turmeric he requested). He has already completed his run in Central Park and his gratitude list in his Day One journal app, and he’s on day 735 following a visit to the Hoffman Institute—the famously mysterious weeklong “personal growth” retreat. “I feel like we’re in The Handmaid’s Tale,” he says. “AI is happening, but we’re going to pretend we’re designing manor houses in the English countryside in the 19th century?” Later he adds, “I have never been interested in recreating the past.”

So when longtime client Rachel Dorrell asked him to create a mountain house in Aspen for her growing family, no one expected a typical ski chalet. From her first visit to the plot of land, she realized the views of the town and the slopes beyond would be unmatched. Today those “crazy views” are framed by windows so large it’s hard to tell where rooms end and the outside begins. De Cárdenas, who collaborated with SAOTA architects to create the modernist structure, set about realizing Dorrell’s vision of “Colorado, but refined,” as she puts it. “Glamorous ’80s apartment” is how he describes the results.

“The mood board was Angelo Donghia,” that great of American design who worked with the likes of Liza Minnelli. “And Beetlejuice, always Beetlejuice! And Mickey Rourke’s apartment in 91/2 Weeks.” A cheetah-print rug sits in the library, while sculpted white custom sofas anchor the immense yet minimalist living room. “So many people have asked, ‘Shouldn’t there be art above the fireplace?’ ” de Cárdenas says. “I’m like, ‘Why?’ ”

Following the rules has never been his style. It has been 20 years since he launched his design firm, after studying at UCLA and assisting architect Greg Lynn at Vienna’s Angewandte institute and MAK museum, then returning to his native New York. The son of an erstwhile fashion PR executive and an actuary, de Cárdenas aspired to work for David Rockwell or for Alex de Betak’s fashion production company, where he once applied for a job. In the end he did a stint at Calvin Klein and worked as a production designer for films and commercials. Then a friend asked for help redoing a kitchen, something he did “kind of at night,” de Cárdenas remembers. Next came his first real client: actress Parker Posey. It was a revelation. “She was the first person I ever had to make window treatments for. I was like, ‘What the f***?’ I remember looking at a window and being like, ‘What is this supposed to look like?’ I didn’t understand that residential interior design is, essentially, fabric.”

Around the same time, he landed his first major corporate client, Nike, and did the design of aNYthing, a Lower East Side boutique that became a nexus for skaters, artists, and musicians in the late 2000s. “No one was doing stores like that at the time,” says creative director Jen Brill, who worked with de Cárdenas on several Nike projects. “There was nothing minimal about it. It was loud, it was fun. It was the first store I saw that was like retail theater.”

Another friend, Felix Burrichter, a curator and the founder of Pin-Up architecture magazine, recalls de Cárdenas’s early fascination with the Memphis Group architects (“when it was still considered bad taste,” Burrichter says) as well as his willingness to experiment with materials, such as applying duct tape patterns to the walls of a Jeffrey Deitch exhibition in Rome, or wrapping an Ikea chair with fluorescent gaffer tape. “For the aNYthing store, I physically built a lot of those things, because there was no one to do it and no budget,” de Cárdenas says. He sometimes laments not being so hands-on anymore, now that he has a team of 18 based in New York and Paris. “There was a lot of energy in those projects.”

“His work is like Limelight–meets–Park Avenue,” says Burrichter, who calls de Cárdenas the heir to ELLE Decor A-List designer Peter Marino, a maestro who works with significant private clients alongside luxury behemoths such as Dior and Chanel. De Cárdenas may have moved from duct tape to gold leaf applied by European artisans, “but there is always that tension in Raf’s work,” Burrichter says, “the binary between decorating and being an architect, between uptown and the club.”

Dorrell, for her part, became aware of de Cárdenas a decade ago, when she and her husband purchased a home in the West Village that he had staged. “We were like, ‘Who did the interiors and who did the art? It’s perfect.’ ” She says they were drawn to the ’70s vibe and the peachy hues, an unusual shade at the time. “We didn’t change a thing.” Since then they have worked together on homes in Austin, New York, and currently their largest yet, in Palm Beach. Dorrell sends “rapid-fire” texts of inspiration ranging from tiled walls at Lake Como’s Villa d’Este to barstools in Paris. “Raf cracks me up—we are brutally honest with each other,” she says, explaining that her taste skews more traditional, while his is often edgier.

“Some of my projects have moments that are ugly, and they excite me,” he says. “Because something truly excellent, something that is pushing boundaries, is not going to land pleasurably at first.” He points to Madonna, a frequent touchstone and source of inspiration for him. “The first time you saw Blond Ambition, the first time you saw Madonna with a high ponytail and with the headset, it was a little jarring. The thick eyebrow and the cone bra.” He often finds himself thinking, Where is that moment now?

Despite his concerns about the flattening effect of the algorithm, he’s still happy he’s in the business of taste. “I can’t believe I was lucky enough to land here, because I didn’t know what interior design even was,” he says. “I like my job. I’m grateful for my job. I want people to say, ‘He’s good at his job.’ ”

The Dorrells might concur: “ ‘We’re not doing another project,’ ” Rachel recalls her husband saying about Aspen. “Now he’s like, ‘Thank god,’ because it’s so awesome.”

#6942. 9 Curb Appeal Trends That Will Take Over in 2027, According to Designers

These are the exterior elements that are getting a major refresh.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Curb appeal is more than having a pretty house.

“It's not just about how a home looks, but how it lives,” says Danielle Faustini, landscape designer and owner of Helix Green Designs. Homeowners and designers alike are stepping up their game when it comes to creating an appealing facade—in some cases, the shift is quite literal, as stairs are becoming an “it” feature.

The latest U.S. Houzz Outdoor Trends study is seeing a shift toward natural elements like wood cladding, beige exteriors, and native plants.

"Professionals on Houzz have shared that homeowners are looking for exteriors that feel grounded and connected to their surroundings," says Annie Thornton, Houzz outdoor trends expert. “Warm beiges and wood siding blend naturally with stone, native plantings and other organic landscape materials, and they suit a wide range of architectural styles. Our studies show that people are moving away from the stark white and cool gray palettes of recent years toward something that feels more timeless, a look that adds a sense of history without tipping into nostalgia.”

Simple upgrades, like a fresh coat of paint on the front door, are also pivoting into colorways anchored in the past. Designer Alexis Warren is seeing a shift toward darker, moodier front doors in shades of green, deep purple, and near-black, like Farrow & Ball’s Paean Black.

Here are 10 curb-appeal-boosting design trends for the year ahead to make your house the envy of the neighborhood.

Introducing a sculptural element—like an outdoor artwork or creatively placed flora—sends a message that your house has a point of view.

“It elevates the sense of arrival and separates your home from other homes in the vicinity,” says designer Lisa Erdmann. To ensure your home truly stands out, look for one-of-a-kind pieces.

For the home pictured above, Robert J. Bell, founder of landscape architecture firm Bell Design, commissioned artisans to create a copper light inspired by a feather. “More than the end of minimalism, the new focus on sculptural shapes brings back a sense of craft, and the expressive and sensory nature that a simple black metal and glass fixture cannot possess,” he says.

To contrast against the patina, Bell chose purple bougainvillea and a sunny yellow paint on the door.

It’s all about adding warmth and texture, and that’s one of the reasons that beige exterior colors have more than doubled in popularity in the last two years, jumping from 10 to 24 percent, according to Houzz.

Think of a palette that extends into the landscape—warm neutrals such as tan, soft cream, khaki, sand, and greige, says Thornton, who also notes that natural wood and wood-look cladding, sandy stucco, and stone are a welcome change.

Adding paneling can mimic the warmth of wood without worrying about upkeep, points out Francisca Alonso, cofounder and CEO of AV Architects + Builders. This trick also helps soften the front elevation. “We like to combine minimalistic modern details with warm tones to make it more inviting and less commercial-looking,” Alonso adds.

A picket fence may be just what your house needs, but that doesn’t mean you have to resort to the predictable white paint.

“Adding a low-profile picket fence is one of the best ways to gently delineate a property line without in any way compromising sightlines of the home,” says Joelle Nesen, principal designer at Maison Inc. “The picket fence has the kind of classical lines that work well with an historic home like this Craftsman in Portland. By not defaulting to the expected white and leaving the fence a natural wood, the picket fence, with its carefully spaced slats that are almost see-through, allows it to blend in with the landscaping and trees that surround the home.”

When you choose gravel over grass or pavers, it’s all about an understated but classy look.

“Gravel and other permeable aggregates like crushed shells are some of my favorite materials for creating more informal outdoor living spaces,” says Faustini. “It is a beautifully tactile and sensory material. Who doesn't love the soft crunch underfoot? And it really softens a space, helping blur the line between outdoor living areas and the surrounding landscape.”

The look works especially well with decking and patinated light fixtures.

Adding native plants around your house feels cohesive, relaxed, and rooted in place, Thornton says.

Rachael Grochowski, founder and principal of RHG Architecture + Design, adds that she expects to see an increase in native landscapes, meadow-type gardens, as well as an integration of clover into lawns.

“The trend to embrace natural landscapes is not only about supporting environment regeneration, but it also supports our wellbeing by instigating wonder, beauty, and randomness,” she says.

Curb appeal is all about first impressions, and homeowners are not taking this feature lightly. Houzz reports that searches for “front walkway” have more than quadrupled (up 357 percent) in the first three quarters of 2026, compared with the same period last year.

Step design, in particular, is one feature that is growing in popularity.

When Sarah Crawford, associate designer with Courtney B. Smith Design, renovated her home, she added steps to her 1920s bungalow to literally and figuratively elevate the experience. The home initially had just one step from the sidewalk to the front door. By adding a series of stairs to gently guide the way to the door, she added a quiet sense of elegance in keeping with the wood-shingled exterior. Key to the transformation was selecting materials that reflect the character and style of the original home, in this case reclaimed granite. Scale and balance were essential to adding a series of stairs to gently guide visitors to the door.

“Steps should never be narrower than the doorway,” Crawford says. “In fact, the wider the steps, the more inviting the approach.”

For a different approach to the front entrance, consider stone pathways, especially those with a casual look, like grass pavers.

Interior designer Darci Hether likes large-format bluestone pavers, which offer a clean, timeless look. “The generous scale of the stones creates a sense of openness, while the greenery between them softens the overall appearance and adds a beautiful, natural element,” she explains.

Erdmann encourages her clients to forgo safe neutrals and treat the front entry door as a place for personality.

“Whether it is hot pink, lipstick red, cobalt blue, or Benjamin Moore’s color of the year, Webster Green—make a statement!” she says. “Don’t match the door to the shutters and choose something fun. A front door is the easiest, lowest-risk place to experiment since it takes one afternoon and a quart of paint.”

Case in point, Elizabeth Cross-Beard, interior designer behind Palm Henri, took to a vibrant peacock blue to accentuate the lines of the above 1925 French manor house. “When paired with natural materials and muted tones, [the paint] brings just enough color to intrigue the eye without disrupting the overall sense of classic grandeur. Unlike other blue tones which can call too much attention to them, this rich color is the front-door sweet spot,” Cross-Beard explains.

Go beyond ho-hum pots that stand sentry at your door. Look for large pieces with personality to anchor the entrance.

“Aged terracotta, weathered stone, or antique urns bring patina and character, helping even a newly built home feel established,” says MK Smith Boykin of Smith Interiors. “With that kind of presence, the planting itself can be quite simple.” The details are especially important when your home has architectural symmetry, like columns.

“Using matching plants only increases the allure of perfect visual balance,” Nesen adds. “The key is that the scale of the planters needs to match the proportions of the door and porch: Selecting pots or containers that are too small will immediately drain the drama and beauty of the front door statement.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6937. Wondering Why Taylor Swift Doesn’t Perform at Festivals Like Coachella? We Did Some Theorizing and Digging

Coachella just released its 2027 lineup, and for those convinced Taylor Swift would be headlining...sorry! While the lineup is absolutely stacked (Madonna! SZA! Dua Lipa!), Taylor is not one of the featured artists. Which...isn’t that surprising. After all, she doesn’t historically do festivals (she is kinda busy casually breaking every touring record and selling out stadiums).

While Taylor hasn’t explained why she’s never headlined Coachella (or other major festival like Glastonbury), it doesn’t seem like a stretch to assume that crowd control could be an issue for her. Taylor is used to performing in the biggest possible stadiums across the world, but they have infrastructure in place to handle crowds even bigger than Coachella, not to mention built-in security. Coachella, as organized as it is, has a somewhat more chaotic vibe, and crowd control is definitely less of a thing.

But if you’re looking for a chatty source who’s commented on the subject, someone told The Huffington Post allllllll the way back in 2014 that “Taylor doesn't like festivals. So for any bookies reading... I think Taylor likes doing her own show because she's in control and it's how she wants it to be. With Glastonbury or any festival, you're at the mercy of the people who run the festival. She wants it all to be perfect.”

Note: Taylor might not like performing at festivals (or at least didn’t in 2014), but she’s definitely a fan in general—she at Travis were spotted at Coachella in 2024 to support her close friend and collaborator Jack Antonoff.

Meanwhile, Taylor did open up about why she’s never performed at the Superbowl halftime show (which fans have also been wondering about), explaining that she’s too locked in on Travis. “That is violent chess,” Tay said of watching football. “That is gladiators without swords. That is dangerous. The whole season I am locked in on what that man is doing on the field. Can you imagine if he's out there every single week putting his life on the line, doing this very dangerous, very high-pressure, high-intensity sport, and I'm like, ‘I wonder what my choreo should be. I think we should do two verses of ‘Shake it Off' into ‘Blank Space’ into ‘Cruel Summer’ would be great.’”

She added that the decision “is nothing to do with Travis, he would love for me to do it. I'm just too locked in.”

Point is: Taylor will likely be sticking to stadium tours, and we’re truly blessed.

#6936. Nicole Kidman’s Long ‘Almond Cream’ Nails Are the Sophisticated Go-With-Everything Trend to Try Now: Get the Look

This manicure proves long almond nails never go out of style.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There are certain manicures that never go out of style, regardless of the season. Case in point: Nicole Kidman’s long ‘almond cream’ nails. Spotted at the LACMA Art+Film Gala wearing softly tapered almond nails in a glossy milky shade, the actor’s manicure served as a reminder that winter manicures needn’t be dark or glittery to make an impact.

“Kidman’s nails are a great example of how to wear a longer length without them looking too dramatic,” says Susan Carroll, founder of Young LDN. “The almond shape is one of my favorites because it’s incredibly flattering on the hands. The sides taper in slightly before finishing in a soft, rounded point, which helps elongate the fingers and gives a much more delicate appearance than a square or coffin shape.”

Serving as an ideal entry point for anyone looking to experiment with longer nails without committing to a dramatic stiletto shape, the almond silhouette offers a more wearable alternative. Carroll also recommends adapting the length to suit your lifestyle and comfort level. The appeal lies in its versatility, as it can be easily achieved without the need for extensions. “The shape does a lot of the work, and keeping the color subtle makes the overall look feel much more elegant,” she adds.

Re-creating the look is very straightforward, as the key lies in the shape. Ask your nail technician for a soft almond silhouette with rounded tips, rather than anything too sharply pointed. “The key is making sure the nails are evenly shaped and the cuticles are tidy, as there’s nowhere to hide imperfections with such a simple manicure,” says Carroll. To finish, simply apply two coats of a sheer milky beige polish before sealing with a glossy top coat for a light-reflecting finish.

The result is an effortlessly elegant manicure that feels equal parts timeless and wearable.

If cooling is a top priority, consider the Oasis Chill, which offers a cooling quilted top for ultimate breathability.

Nectar’s Black Friday sale is officially here, and you can save up to 50% on mattresses and 66% off bundles. Our editors are fans of Nectar’s vast memory foam and hybrid offerings, especially for those looking for something budget-friendly.

Assistant beauty editor Jasmine Hyman has the Premier Copper Hybrid model and says “It’s perfectly plush without feeling like I’m going to sink through—while still providing ample support for my body throughout the night. I also love that you can’t feel any coils or springs while you lie down or move around on the mattress.”

Tuft & Needle is known for its Mint mattress, which stands out for its buoyant and bouncy construction that’s noticeably cooling (thanks to gel-infused foams that absorb heat). Its array of mattresses is ideal for back and side sleepers, or anyone who prefers a medium-firm feel. Right now, the brand’s mattresses and bedding are 20% off, so you can shop everything from cooling gel-infused memory foam mattresses to responsive hybrid models without paying full price.

When it comes to scoring home essentials at a great price, Wayfair is one of our editors’ go-to sites. Leading up to Black Friday, Wayfair’s sale section is already brimming with amazing deals on furniture and mattresses; we found markdowns of up to 61% off Sealy and Wayfair’s in-house mattress brand.

#6924. The New South of France

The French Riviera is always a good idea. Visit in the off-season to soak in its buzzy contemporary-art scene sans tourists.

The South of France became a see-and-be-seen summer destination a century ago, when the free-spirited American couple Gerald and Sara Murphy began hosting epic beach parties in Cap d’Antibes. At any one of them, you might find Jean Cocteau and Igor Stravinsky unpacking a picnic basket, Pablo Picasso or Ernest Hemingway flirting with Sara (who famously wore pearls with her bathing suit), or F. Scott Fitzgerald taking mental notes for Tender Is the Night.

The legacies of the Lost Generation are still a thrill to follow, though booking a room at the Hotel du Cap-Eden-Roc, where the Murphys liked to stow their guests, is now a trickier prospect. The French Riviera remains a wildly popular summer destination and will serve as the next setting for HBO’s The White Lotus. Instead, visit in fall or even winter, when you’ll have space to wander through the Picasso museum in Antibes’s Grimaldi Castle or take in Cocteau’s murals and frescoes in the Chapelle Saint-Pierre in Villefranche-sur-Mer or in the Villa Santo Sospir in Saint-Jean-Cap-Ferrat (which was recently renovated and is available to rent).

But what makes the South of France even more compelling now is the thriving contemporary-art scene, ignited, in part, by the collector Maja Hoffmann, who opened the dynamic museum complex LUMA Arles, with its Frank Gehry tower, in 2021. She has also helped create some of the city’s most fashionable hotels: L’Arlatan, with its tiled interiors by artist Jorge Pardo; the Grand Hôtel Nord-Pinus, reenvisioned by designer Duro Olowu; and Le Cloître, designed by India Mahdavi. (The latter closes for the winter after November 29.) “The energy around LUMA has drawn such an interesting mix of people that I sometimes prefer Arles to Paris,” says Mahdavi, who has a home there.

The winery estate Château La Coste, near Aix-en-Provence, boasts one of the most ambitious art-focused hospitality projects in the region, featuring site-specific works from Tadao Ando, Oscar Niemeyer, and Matisse’s grandson Paul.

This past spring, everyone was talking about the opening of the Zannier Ile de Bendor, a new resort located on the private island Ile de Bendor, a 10-minute boat ride from the coastal village of Bandol. Reopening after the winter season this May, it features 93 rooms, a 13,000-square-foot wellness center, an art gallery, and three ateliers that will host a rotating cast of artisans.

In Marseille, the seaside Tuba Club is a favorite of the creative set. There’s also Hôtel Amista, a new 24-room property near the Old Port reimagined by Dorothée Delaye. The can’t-miss shopping destination here is unquestionably Maison Empereur, a hardware store dating back to 1827 with a trove of one-of-a-kind home goods.

Lastly, in rural Provence, Cosima Ungaro, daughter of couturier Emanuel Ungaro, has reinvented two of the buildings on her late father’s medieval estate, La Cavalerie, into spaces for artists and travelers. “The light, the stars, the wind—these elements have exerted a magnetic pull on artists for centuries,” she says.

This story initially appeared in the March 2026 issue of Harper’s Bazaar. It has been updated to reflect seasonal changes.

#6921. All About Anthony De La Torre, Lana Condor’s Actor/Musician Husband

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

In the To All the Boys films, Lana Condor’s character is often consumed by relationship drama. In any given movie, Lara Jean may be torn between her current boyfriend Peter Kavinsky (Noah Centineo) and childhood crush John Ambrose McClaren (Jordan Fisher), or struggling with whether to follow Peter to college. But in real life, Condor faces no such dilemma. She’s been in a relationship with actor/musician Anthony De La Torre since 2015. In January 2022, the two got engaged, and in October 2024, they got married. Below, all you need to know about De La Torre and his romance with Condor.

Like Condor, De La Torre is an actor who most famously played a young Jack Sparrow in 2017’s Pirates of the Caribbean: Dead Men Tell No Tales. He’s also appeared in episodes of the Nickelodeon series 100 Things to Do Before High School and the Starz show Vida.

The couple actually met at an entertainment industry function. During her February 2019 cover story with Cosmopolitan, Condor spoke about meeting De La Torre at an Emmy nominees reception in 2015. His pickup line? “Hi, I’m Anthony. I thought I would make a friend.” She described their initial meeting to the outlet as being “like a ­Taylor Swift song.”

Ever since their storybook meeting, De La Torre and Condor have stayed by each other’s side. The actress told Cosmopolitan UK that her beau is quite the romantic. “He writes me love letters all the time,” she told the outlet. “He’s the freaking best. He’ll leave me little messages taped to my bathroom mirror on days I have to wake up early for work. He once picked up my favorite takeout food after work and served it up properly on our kitchen table, with little sticky notes attached to each plate saying how proud of me he is.”

Both Condor and De La Torre wrote romantic Instagram posts to celebrate their fifth anniversary as a couple. In August 2020, she shared a photo of the pair cuddled up on the grass with the caption, “5 years with the man of my dreams. You are still my most favorite person in the world. You are my best friend. You have taught me how to love and to be loved, to forgive and to grow, to never give up, to be each others teammate and cheerleader. You make me laugh like no other and it doesn’t matter where we are in the world, as long as I’m with you, I’m home. I love you @anthonydltorre happy anniversary. Let’s cuddle. 🐶”

De La Torre’s post, also shared on August 31, includes a sunset picture. He wrote, “5 years ago we went on our first date and saw a movie called The Gift. While the plot of the film in no way represents our life, the name of the movie couldn’t be more fitting to what you are. The greatest gift I've ever been blessed and entrusted with. From our tiny ass apartment with a blow up twin mattress to riding horses in Kenya, it has always been an adventure 🤪 Thank you for changing my life, inspiring me to grow, and making everything worth it. I love you, my tiny burrito 🌯♥️”

Condor shared the news that De La Torre popped the question on Jan. 28, 2022. She explained the details behind the ring and shared engagement photos, which you can view here on her Instagram.

She wrote, of the proposal, “Saying YES was the easiest decision I’ve ever made. I consider myself the absolute luckiest woman alive to live in your sphere. Aside from my father, without a doubt, you are the greatest man in the world. Emmy & Timmy said it’s about time mommy & daddy got engaged!!!”

Condor added of the ring, “💍: Anthony worked with the INCREDIBLE Vietnamese female owned @parisjewellerscanada to design the most stunning piece I’ve ever seen! The fact he knew how important it was to me that he’d collaborate w/ a Vietnamese company to help design my ring is a testament to just how truly thoughtful he always is. I cannot wait to be your wife, baby. I love you a million times over.”

The couple got married in Malibu at the Serra Franciscan Retreat Center, according to Vogue. Condor told the publication that she and her late mother Mary went to a spiritual retreat there when she was in high school.

“My mother Mary recently passed away, leaving my entire family devastated,” the actress shared. “But she would not have wanted us to not celebrate our love. We really wanted to get married at the Serra Retreat because being there was such a core memory that I had with my mom, and I felt like we would be able to feel her presence on our wedding night.”

The bride wore a strapless Vera Wang dress she picked out with her mother as well.

“My mom and I absolutely fell in love with my wedding gown the moment I tried it on, and I am so grateful I got to share that experience with her,” she said. “That was the only time she was able to see me in my dress, and I will hold that memory in my heart forever.”

She added, “The entire weekend could not have gone better. We are so filled with love and gratitude for each other and also for all of our incredible guests who came from far and wide to join us in the celebrations. This weekend will forever go down as the happiest weekend of our lives.”

The couple kicked off the year at Vanity Fair and Amazon MGM Studios’ awards season celebration in Los Angeles on January 10.

Condor wore a black Sebastian Gunawan Signature off-the-shoulder gown, while De La Torre opted for a copper-colored suit.

On March 15, they attended the Elton John AIDS Foundation’s annual Academy Awards viewing party in West Hollywood. Condor chose an ivory Alberta Ferretti halter-neck gown, while De La Torre wore a light blue suit.

They stepped out again on July 28 for a Los Angeles screening of The Devil’s Mouth, with Condor wearing a red gown featuring a plunging, draped neckline and De La Torre dressed in a black suit.

The pair also attended the US Open in New York City on September 8. Condor wore a navy top layered over a plaid collared shirt, accessorized with pink-tinted sunglasses, while De La Torre wore a blue blazer over a checked shirt.

In a Nov. 10, 2026 interview with People, Condor and De La Torre revealed that they spent their second wedding anniversary filming The Receipt, an upcoming romantic comedy that lets them share a love story on-screen, too.

“It was so cute. We got to celebrate it on set together with Fetch,” Condor said. “I don’t think we really told anyone. I think they just kind of knew and they were so, so sweet and everyone on set was just the most adorable. It was the cutest love-filled day. It was delightful.”

While they’ve worked together on music before, playing a couple on-screen was a new experience for Condor.

“I’ve fallen in love on camera quite a few times, but I think it’s a lot different when you can actually do it with someone that you share your life with and that you’ve loved for a very long time,” she said.

In addition to being an up-and-coming actor, De La Torre is also a musician. He’s previously been in bands including DE LA TORRE and The Fell. In October 2019, he released a new song titled “Know Me.” According to Seventeen, the track was written about Condor.

De La Torre and Condor teased the release of a joint musical project on Jan. 7, 2020. She shared a photo of the couple snuggled near a laptop on Instagram and captioned it, “We’ve been working on a very special project for months now for you guys. It’s been blood, sweat, and tears. It’s been highs & lows & sobs & laughs but we’ve done it together, and that’s what counts 💪🏽. Building dreams together. Love your loved ones, love your strangers. We can’t wait to share with you so soon x.”

Speculation that the couple was working on music together grew after De La Torre’s cryptic Instagram post. “Feb 19,” he captioned a shot of the pair.

The answer to questions about Condor and De La Torre’s first collaboration arrived with the dreamy duet, “Raining in London.” Condor announced the release of their song (and De La Torre’s EP) on Instagram. “This is our heart,” she wrote. “Our song 'Raining In London' (ft. me) is FINALLY OUT, and so is my dear @anthonydltorre whole EP FIND ME. I couldn’t be more proud of him. Against so many odds, he’s made this EP come to life for you all to hear. It’s definitely playing on repeat all day for me 💕! I love you golden boy, thru and thru.”

De La Torre also has his own channel, which includes a video of fun facts about the actor. In it, he opens up about learning to speak Spanish, his close bond with his siblings, and why he once dyed his hair black.

In the months since their first duet, the couple recorded a cover of Lauv’s “I Like Me Better” and released another original song, “No Way.” They even choreographed a TikTok dance for the latter song.

As for Condor’s musical catalog, she has also drawn inspiration from De La Torre. Last August, she released the song “For Real,” a track about how much she misses her longtime boyfriend while traveling for work.

“I’ve been in a pretty serious relationship for five years now, and whenever I travel I have to leave him,” she explained to People. “I always have a lot of anxiety about going off and leaving my home. I had just written down this journal entry about my feelings of going back to work as things start to shoot again, and my anxieties about leaving my boyfriend and about starting life up again, especially in this insane year that we've been having.”

Condor went on to address her new musical collaborations with De La Torre: “I mean, it seems so random, but I’ve always loved music. My boyfriend is a musician, and I’ve watched him navigate the music industry for years now, and I have always been very fascinated with it. I just felt like during quarantine, why not try something new if you love it?”

Condor told Cosmopolitan that when To All the Boys I’ve Loved Before was released in 2018, rumors about her and Centineo’s offscreen relationship persisted. “Noah and I definitely encouraged the speculation,” she admitted. “But it’s because we believe in the story and the characters, and we genuinely love each other. You can truly love someone in a very platonic way.”

But Condor said Instagram trolls would say “horrible things” about her real-life romance with De La Torre. “It was so hurtful for both of us,” she explained. “This is supposed to be a good thing. Why are you ­coming for someone who has no part of this story? If you say you support me, why would you hurt someone I love?”

For a while, De La Torre disabled his Instagram comments. But now, the couple seems to be handling social media pressure in stride. In August 2019, De La Torre even jokingly shared a photo with himself and Centineo, followed by a pic of Condor and co-star Madelaine Arthur. He captioned the post, “PK and LJ are even cuter in person.”

When De La Torre isn’t writing Condor love letters or sonnets, he’s providing another public service. Namely, he’s sharing photos with his and Condor’s dog Emmy. Yes, she has an Instagram:

Each couple experienced isolation a little differently during the COVID-19 pandemic. For Condor and De La Torre, they tried out TikTok trends and wore matching tie-dye outfits. Last March, both of the performers shared videos of themselves experimenting on the app to Instagram.

#6920. An Inside Look at Cosmopolitan x Lionsgate’s ‘Sunrise on the Reaping’ Screening

Friends and fans of ‘The Hunger Games’ came together for early screenings of the franchise’s latest prequel.

There’s no doubt that The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping is one of the buzziest movie releases of the year. #6919. ¡Vamos, España!

With Spain fresh off a World Cup win, there’s no better time to visit Madrid and the cooler northern regions of Asturias and Galicia

I lived in Spain in the early aughts, when the country was experiencing a mic-drop moment. The shiny, futuristic Frank Gehry–designed Guggenheim Museum had gone up in Bilbao a few years earlier, setting off what is now known as the Bilbao effect: a phenomenon that describes how investing in a visionary architecture project can transform a place. Food-obsessed travelers were flocking to a small beach town in a remote corner of northern Spain just to experience a meal at El Bulli, the legendary, now closed, three-Michelin-star restaurant headed up by Ferran Adrià.

Spain is now in the midst of another glow-up, thanks in part to its World Cup win, and Madrid is front and center. A recent influx of expats from South and North America has locals calling the city the “Miami of Europe.” But don’t let that dissuade you. Mallorquín designer Rosa Esteva, who designs the elegant, made-in-Spain fashion brand Cortana, emphasizes “the charm of those glorious old places that have survived through time.” That means staying at hotels like the intimate Hotel Orfila, booking a table at La Parra, wandering through El Rastro, Madrid’s legendary flea market, and checking out a late-night flamenco show at Tablao de la Villa. At the same time, contemporary places like Esteva’s new shop in the Salamanca neighborhood, the modern tapas bar Sala de Despiece, and the contemporary-art gallery Albarrán Bourdais are worth checking out.

The typical Spanish itinerary often includes a few days in Barcelona to take in the Gaudí before heading south to the beaches of Andalusia. But Vella González, the founder of fashion brand Boxcalf, which has a flagship in Madrid’s Malasaña neighborhood, extols northern Spain’s charms and cooler climate. “For non-Spaniards, it’s an undiscovered paradise,” she says. Start in San Sebastián, one of the world’s most celebrated gastronomic capitals, and eat your way through its pintxos bars, finishing with a steak at the Basque grill house Portuetxe.

In Bilbao, discover the next generation of art spaces, such as the light-filled expansion of the Bilbao Fine Arts Museum, designed by Norman Foster and Luis María Uriarte. The Bilbao effect can be felt in nearby Santander, thanks to the new Faro Santander museum and cultural space, a historic building reimagined by David Chipperfield and currently showcasing the works of surrealist painter Leonora Carrington. Move on to the pristine beaches, green hills, and exceptional dining scene of the Asturias and Galicia regions. In Asturias, splurge on a stay at the remote wabi-sabi styled Solo Palacio and explore the old-growth forests and prehistoric rock shelters of Las Ubiñas-La Mesa Natural Park. Cozy up in the Casa Beatnik Country House hotel in Galicia, and walk the last 10 miles of the famed Camino de Santiago trail to Santiago de Compostela and its Old Town, a UNESCO World Heritage site. It’s an idyllic ending to a new kind of Spanish pilgrimage.

#6916. My Favorite Travel Blow Dryer Is Under $100 Right Now

The Dreame Pocket Cake Hair Dryer is pink, powerful, and folds up to the size of an e-reader.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

My first attempt to travel internationally with a hot tool ended in tragedy. I was on a student exchange program in Spain, naively unaware that the outlet voltage in Europe is not the same as in the U.S. Eager to put my best strands forward for my first day in Almería, I plugged in my brand new Paul Mitchell flat iron... which sparked once and never turned on again. Eighteen years later, dual voltage capability is thankfully fairly standard in stylers, so I moved on to a new struggle: finding a travel blow dryer worth the luggage space.

Right before an October trip to Italy, my hot tool prayers were answered in the form of an email pitch. Following in the footsteps of another vacuum company that starts with D, home technology brand Dreame has is getting into the hair styling business. Its most recent beauty launch is the Pocket Cake Hair Dryer, a travel-size blow dryer that comes in “three macaron-inspired shades.” I could tell from the press release that the tool was very cute, but I’ve had enough experience with travel dryers that lack power to be skeptical. I called in a sample, threw it in my checked bag, and put it to the test in Bologna.

Reader, I was thrilled with the results. In honor of the Pocket Cake Hair Dryer being on sale for most of November, here’s my full review.

On top of the international outlet capabilities, the I’m looking for three things in a travel blow dryer: It needs to be lightweight (for packing purposes), close to as powerful as my at-home tool (I’m not trying to spend more time doing my hair on vacation), and it needs to have a diffuser attachment (I usually wear my natural waves when I’m traveling). I was please to find the Pocket Cake does all three.

The brand claims that the Pocket Cake folds down to the size of a phone, to which I say, “Wow, you guys must have massive phones.” It is comparable in size to my Kindle Paperwhite, which is still pretty small by hair dryer standards.

I don’t own a scale so I can’t verify the brand’s claim that this weighs less than a pound, but it’s definitely lightweight. The set also comes with a smoothing and diffusing attachment, both of which are slightly smaller than a full-size version and also don’t weigh much. There’s also a nice zip up bag included; I packed everything inside of that bag and it fit easily into my checked luggage.

In terms of power, the brand says that this tool is capable of drying short hair “in as little as 40 seconds.” My hair is pretty long and medium density, but I still felt like this worked about as quickly as the blow dryers I use at home: To get to 70% dry (my preferred stopping point when I’m wearing my waves) it took about seven minutes. The diffuser adds weight, so I’d say that in the end it was comparable to how heavy my home dryers are too.

Another claim is that this dryer has “350 million plasma ions to help reduce static and smooth flyaways.” I’m not totally clear on what a plasma ion is, nor is “smoothing” generally the goal when I’m using a diffuser, but my hair did look nice and shiny once it had fully dried.

Best of all: No outlet meltdown. My high school trauma is finally healed!

Overall, I honestly have no notes on this device. It’s everything I need in a travel hair dryer (and leaps and bounds better than anything you’ll find in a hotel bathroom) and very cute to boot. If you’re looking for a compact, lightweight blow dryer with a diffuser attachment to bring on your next trip, I highly suggest scooping this up. Which leads me to...

The Dreame Pocket Cake usually goes for $129.99, but it’s on sale for most of November. From now through the 18th, you can snag it for $$109.99; from the 19th to the 30th (I guess Black Friday is 11 days long now?) you can get it for $99.99. I think it’s definitely worth the full price, but even better to get it for under a hundred bucks.

Kara McGrath is a contributing beauty editor at Cosmo. She has over 15 years of experience as a beauty reporter, a job that’s included testing as many new hot tools as possible every year.

Kara McGrath is a writer and editor with over 17 years of experience covering the beauty industry, women’s health, and a wide range of other lifestyle and wellness topics. After most recently serving as content director at Allure (2019-2025) and executive editor at Bustle (2014-2019), she is now fully freelance. In addition to Cosmo, her writing has appeared in Allure, The New York Times, Marie Claire, Business of Fashion, Playboy, NH Magazine, and more. Kara lives in New Hampshire with her dog, four cats, and a rotating cast of foster animals. You can find more of her work at karamcgrath.com.

#6911. Winter’s Most Wanted: Tommy Hilfiger Holiday Picks for Every Occasion

From sharp knits to travel-ready extras, these are the pieces worth putting on your list.

The sweater that looked fine in October suddenly feels too thin, the jacket you reach for every day isn’t warm enough, and a weekend away calls for more than whatever you can squeeze into an overnight bag. Then there’s the rest of the season: office parties, cold walks across town, slow mornings at home, and the occasional trip out of town. Getting dressed for all of it takes a few reliable essentials.

Tommy Hilfiger has been building its take on American style since 1985, when the brand launched with a fresh spin on classic menswear. Its signature mix of preppy staples, sporty details, and relaxed ease has made it a year-round proposition, with pieces that move from everyday dressing to more seasonal needs. For winter, that means familiar wardrobe staples with a little extra warmth, practical layers for unpredictable weather, and accessories suited to life on the go. These six picks cover the season from the first cold snap to the last weekend getaway. Here’s what we’re putting on our fashion gift list.

A cable-knit sweater is a cold-weather staple, and Tommy Hilfiger’s take keeps the look classic. Made from combed cotton with a traditional cable pattern and ribbed trim, it’s an easy layer under a wool coat or a natural choice for a casual holiday gathering. Pair it with dark jeans or chinos for a straightforward winter look.

For trips, a jacket that barely takes up any room in your bag is always useful. Tommy Hilfiger’s lightweight puffer folds into its own interior zip pocket, making it easy to stash for a weekend away or keep on hand when the forecast is unpredictable. It has thermal insulation, a regular fit, and side pockets, so it’s a practical layer for travel and everyday errands alike.

A good winter evening often calls for staying in, and this sleep set makes a comfortable night at home feel a little more put together. This one pairs a simple T-shirt with plaid flannel pants, bringing a classic cold-weather pattern into the mix. It’s an easy gift for someone who appreciates a slow weekend morning as much as a cozy night in.

A warm beanie is one of winter’s most useful accessories, and this one gets an upgrade with a cotton-cashmere blend. The classic knit is an easy finishing touch for everything from a morning commute to a weekend walk. It’s also a practical gift that will see plenty of use once the temperature drops.

A card holder and keychain are the kind of everyday essentials that make an especially easy gift. Tommy Hilfiger’s leather set brings the two together in one package, with a polished finish that feels a step up from the usual stocking stuffer. It’s a useful pick for someone who likes to keep their daily carry simple.

A weekend away calls for a bag that can handle a change of clothes and a few extra essentials without looking like you’re heading to the gym. Tommy’s leather duffel brings a more polished feel to the overnight bag, making it a smart companion for a winter getaway or a quick trip home for the holidays.

#6909. Megan Skiendel Sat Down to Perform at a KATSEYE Concert While Recovering From a Concussion

The girlies of KATSEYE are still on the road for their Wildworld Tour after reaching another career milestone at last month’s Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show, and their latest show looked a little different for Megan Skiendel.

During their November 10 tour stop at the Moody Center in Austin, Texas, Megan performed sitting on the side of the stage in sunglasses as the remaining members, Daniela Avanzini, Lara Raj, and Yoonchae, pushed through the show’s regularly scheduled programming.

It was Megan’s first time on stage since recovering from a concussion, and based on these fan-captured clips, it seems like all she wants to do is dance again.

Of course, Eyekons remained concerned about Megan’s health during the performance.

“I can’t believe this is the state of KATSEYE right now,” one person wrote, while another added that her condition should be taken more seriously.

“like that's gonna do the job........ girl say goodbye to your brain and hello to CTE like it IS that serious,” they wrote. “fire whoever allowed her on the stage and cancel this fuck ass tour like this is crazy...”

Megan’s sit-down moment on stage comes after she confirmed she was returning with limitations.

“I wanted to give you a little update and let you know that while I’m feeling so much better, I am still actively recovering from my concussion so I’ll need to take some precautions as I continue to heal,” she wrote in a statement shared on KATSEYE’s November 9 Instagram Story.

“I won't be going full out just yet!” she added, explaining that she will have to be reassessed by a doctor before she can return to “unrestricted performances.”

“That said, I’m so excited to be back on stage with the girls and see all of you in Austin! I’m incredibly grateful for the opportunity to be there, even if I have to do things a little differently this time,” she wrote.

The global girl group seems to be persevering through the tour since it kicked off its North American leg on October 13. Not only are two members, Manon Bannerman and Sophia Laforteza, absent from performances, but several shows have been canceled or postponed due to illness. The Grammy nominees previously canceled tour stops in Detroit, Minneapolis, and Chicago.

“The label has made the decision to postpone our shows in Chicago and Minneapolis as Megan is recovering from a concussion,” the group wrote in its Instagram Story on November 5. “We are incredibly sorry to disappoint our fans. We know how much you’ve been looking forward to these shows, and that many of you have traveled long distances to be there. We don’t take that lightly, and we’re truly sorry for the disappointment.”

“We’ll announce the rescheduled dates as soon as possible, so please hold on to your tickets,” they continued. “We can’t wait to see you in both cities again soon. Thank you for your continued love, support and understanding.”

Wishing Megan and the girls nothing but the best as she continues her recovery!

#6905. 27 Creative Ways Designers Hide TVs for a Stylish, Screen-Free Environment

These genius tricks keep it accessible yet out of sight.

For years, many designers and design-loving homeowners have felt that having the TV on display is a major faux pas. Even though most of us have one and use it daily, there’s something unappealing about having a big black box against the wall and breaking up the interior flow. It’s become even more of an eyesore now that analog spaces are becoming more popular.

While one solution is to invest in a TV that can disguise itself as artwork, those come at a high price; the other option is to learn how to hide the TV in a way that doesn’t feel obvious or tacky, and thankfully, designers have a lot of ideas.

We’ve also included plenty of inspiring photos and expert tips to guide you along the way. If your goal is to reduce the visual presence of technology in your home, you’re in the right place. Keep reading to find inventive ways to make any room feel screen-free—at least when the TV isn’t in use.

Of course, you can hide your television the same way you'd hide anything else you wouldn't want on full display at all times: tuck it into a cabinet. At least, that's what designer Kylie Buhagiar at Melbourne, Australia-based Golden did. "In the main living room, the TV is discreetly integrated into a wall-hung metal joinery box, with hinged doors that elegantly stack back to reveal the screen when needed, maintaining a clean and uncluttered aesthetic," Buhagiar says. "This thoughtful design transforms the joinery into a sculptural piece of furniture, allowing the space to effortlessly transition from a main living room to a calm sitting area, enhancing the home's rich yet comfortable atmosphere."

The whole point of concealing the TV is to hide it, but sometimes, the mechanism used to hide the screen just draws more attention to the area. Gillian Segal, an interior designer based in Vancouver, British Columbia, came up with a clever solution that basically looks like the wall. "This room functioned as both a formal living space and informal family room (complete with movie nights and lots of kids' TV shows), so we wanted a design that felt both elegant and approachable," Segal explains. "We concealed the TV behind a very simple millwork door system that folds open and closed. It was a cost-effective solution that allowed other elements in the room to shine."

It’s no secret that many designers find the Samsung Frame TV to be beautiful, but designer Ariel Okin takes it a step further by adding a literal frame to the Frame. This hidden-TV tactic ensures that it matches other elements in the space, such as the fireplace screen, thus hiding it in plain sight.

If you don’t want to think about your TV when it’s off, you can cleverly camouflage it. In this living room, designer Emily Del Bello painted a shadowy accent wall black and mounted the TV there—you wouldn't even notice it at first glance.

In a room as traditional and cohesive as this, you probably don't want a big black rectangle interrupting your aesthetic, so do what designer Lynne Uhalt did and hide your TV in a closet. This guest room is heavily inspired by European interiors and architecture, but Uhalt still wanted the guests to be able to watch TV and relax in bed. As a design-friendly solution, she put the TV in one of the closets, which still boasts enough room to enough to hold clothes.

Designer John Wooden maximized limited square footage in his clients' cottage by cleverly hiding several items, including placing their TV in a beautiful credenza. One of the simplest methods to hide your TV is by incorporating it into furniture that already complements the space. This wooden mid-century piece fits perfectly into the room, making it an ideal spot for a hidden TV.

In a maximalist, highly colorful space like this, designer Shazalynn Cavin-Winfrey couldn't see how a TV could work in the room, so she decided to hide it in the green cabinet in the back. However, the designer took it a step further and covered the glass with fabric that pulls hues from the rest of the room.

Everick Brown designed a statement wall encased in custom steel with walnut insets to create a “floating credenza” for a hidden TV. A console table behind the sofa helps separate the two living room zones.

Designed to look like abstract artwork, Kristin Fine hid the TV behind modern paneling above the fireplace. It suits the neutral tones nicely and maintains the refined and grown-up atmosphere while also ensuring that it's family-friendly.

This living room sitting area revolves around the corner fireplace. To ensure that the TV didn't take up too much valuable visual space, designer Denise McGaha invested in a Samsung Frame and disguised it as a photograph that complements the blue and grey color scheme.

This outdoor living room, designed by Amanda Lindroth, is the perfect place to entertain and unwind. Look closely at the pagoda-shaped cabinet above the fireplace, and you'll notice that it opens to reveal a hidden TV. It was based on a design from St. Michael's Mount castle in England.

What do you do in a busy kitchen where the children love TV and the mom doesn't—but she does want to keep an eye on said kids? Enter the Samsung Frame, once again making it all work in family homes with competing interests and preferences, like in April Tomlin Interiors designer Sarah Rogers's kitchen here.

A bi-fold panel painting by Stuart Coleman Budd hides a TV, but the goal was to be transparent. “Bronze hinges honestly express that it’s a movable screen—that this is true kinetic art,” says architect Ken Pursley of Pursley Dixon Architecture.

This formal living room, designed by Mark Cunningham, doubles as a more relaxed and casual hangout room. To ensure that it could do both well, Cunningham built a hidden TV into the back wall shelf that pops up when the occupants want to unwind with a good movie.

Don’t hide it, just make it one with your wall. This gallery wall of various-sized framed prints blends in with the Samsung Frame TV, which has a wood frame and a rotating display of art images. “None of my clients' guests realize it's a TV when they first walk into the room,” says Natalie Myer of Veneer Designs.

Custom paneling over a fireplace elevates the television area in this bedroom lounge zone by Studio McGee. Flush with the fireplace facade and further disguised by a custom mirror, the hidden TV definitely will not be ruining the chic decor scheme here.

Related Story: Shea McGee Just Dropped a Cottagecore Home Collab With a Major Fashion Brand

Designer CeCe Barfield Thompson hid a TV behind a retractable antique mirror for maximum glamour. “In an oak-paneled Manhattan library, I designed an antique mirror to hide the wall-mounted television,” Thompson says. “The mirror's lower panels retract like a garage door to reveal an entertainment system behind. This mechanism allowed us to create a room that was incredibly functional, without sacrificing an ounce of beauty!”

Related Story: This Manhattan Apartment Showcases an Abundance of Antiques and Contemporary Art

Here's another example of custom cabinetry with a hidden TV. A sliding door flush with the wood accent wall makes this media room designed by Heidi Caillier extra discreet and pretty. After all, the custom upholstery, drapery, and grasscloth wallpaper are too good to get lost in the entertainment system shuffle.

Designer Nina Farmer took the most elegant approach possible—hiding the TV in a custom mirrored cabinet atop the mantel. “The living room needed a concealed TV due to the formality of the architecture,” she says. “Hand-blown mirror was used on the doors so that the enclosure fit with the original 1850s marble mantel. It has concealed hinges and no pull, so when it’s closed, you would never know what’s behind it.”

If you can’t outright conceal your screen, the key is to avoid the “black hole” effect when it’s off. “I always try to blend them into the architecture of the house,” says designer Eche Martinez. “We recently completed a project in Belvedere, CA, where the homeowners were hesitant about installing a TV in their living room. To solve this, we decided that the best way to divert attention from the TV when it was not in use was to have an oversized, freestanding piece of art right next to it. Clients loved it, and above all, the piece looks great in the room."

#6904. Angelina Jolie Masters Timeless Autumnal Layering at Dinner With Sons Pax and Knox

The leaves are changing, the temperatures are cooling, and it’s finally time for wool coats. After weeks of feeling like summer would never let up, we’ve officially transitioned into the cozy season—just ahead of everyone’s favorite autumnal holiday. And last night, some two weeks ahead of Thanksgiving, Angelina Jolie got the family time started.

The actor was spotted grabbing dinner with sons Pax and Knox at West Hollywood hotspot Cecconi’s. She looked effortlessly refined for the outing, bundling up in layers that included a loose white button-down shirt and a brown wool coat.

Her wide-leg gray trousers, designed with a slouchy, relaxed silhouette, reached all the way to the ground, covering her black pointed-toe footwear.

Jolie, who shares Pax, Knox, and their four siblings with ex-husband Brad Pitt, wore the classic, comfy ensemble with black sunglasses and an ornate silver pendant necklace crafted with delicate filigree work.

Next to her, Pax—aged 22—wore a varsity-inspired navy-blue jacket over a white T-shirt and dark jeans. His black-and-white sneakers appeared to be a pair of low tops from xVessel’s collaboration with Yohji Yamamoto, recognizable for its chunky, deconstructed sole.

Knox, on the other hand, went for a lighter look with a white T-shirt from Shihiko, embroidered with the phrase, “It’s been a long day.” The experimental piece, worn here without a coat, was printed with a hidden UV reactive graphic of a cat, which only appeared under sunlight. The 18-year-old tucked the tee into brown trousers and finished things off with a pop of yellow, by way of his smiley cap-toe Prospective Flow sneakers.
