#6902. The 12 Best Puffer Jackets You Can Buy Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We love a barn jacket. We love a blazer. But when it’s below freezing and snowing sideways, neither is going to do you much good. That’s puffer jacket weather, and no number of layered sweaters under your favorite jacket is going to change the forecast.

Luckily, there’s no shortage of puffer jackets out there. The trick is finding one that’s warm enough, durable enough, and ready to stand up to whatever precipitation the winter sky throws its way—all while looking good enough that you don’t feel like the Michelin Tire Man every time you leave the house. The good news? There are plenty of puffer jackets that fit the bill, from luxury options from Canada Goose and Moncler to more affordable ones from REI and Carhartt.

There is a range in how exactly a puffer gets its shape, too. Most are down-filled, and the way the stitching is designed affects the puffiness. The fewer the stitches, the bigger the puffs. For something more packed down, there are ones with denser stitching that make for a slimmer-fitting jacket. Then you get into the nitty-gritty: Is the hood removable? Can you wash it at home? Is it really, truly, waterproof? Start your shopping with these puffer jackets you can buy right now and wear for many cold days to come.

One of the most iconic purveyors of the puffer jacket, TNF is a brand that deserves the hype it gets. This style, with its wide puffs, is a throwback look that’s oh so welcome in the 2020s. It's designed to tough out even the coldest winter days, with a water-repellent and windproof outer, goose down, and a three-piece hood that you can stow away into the collar.

To me, this is the perfect packable puffer: Slim enough to roll up and stash away and sturdy enough to brave wind, rain, and snow. REI designs this lightweight style for the man on the go, so for all your winter hikes and outdoor excursions, this is the perfect in-between option that won't weigh you down.

Canada Goose is a household name when it comes to winter gear, and the Lawrence Puffer is one of its best sellers for good reason. Our editors (along with actors like Daniel Craig and Ethan Hawke) love this lightweight, warm, cozy jacket, which was designed with the details in mind; it has a suede chin guard so the stand collar won’t bother you, backpack straps, reflective stripes, and even two different color options for the disc logo.

Eddie Bauer’s CirrusLite is a sleeper hit. It won’t win awards as the most wind-resistant or coolest looking, though I love the more earthy colorways. It layers well, keeps you warm, and is durable enough if you need to wear it on a legit mountain—all this while being super lightweight and easy to pack.

Whether you're commuting in a bustling city or packing for a cold-weather trip, this lightweight jacket is a winter staple. It's stylish but doesn't skimp on insulation for a chilly day. The best part, though: Unlike many other puffers, this one is about as slim fitting as you can get. Kiss all the extra bulk goodbye this winter. Or, if temperatures go below freezing, add a wool coat on top for extra coverage.

Not only is this jacket insulated, but the interior is lined with sherpa, too, making it a cold-weather superhero. The high collar and full-front zip make it virtually windproof, so whether you’re darting from building to building or staying outside for a while, you’re all set.

Don’t get me wrong, an Arc’teryx Thorium is a phenomenal jacket. I just think at that $450 price point, you’re often just as good going for a North Face Nuptse (if you want an outer layer) or an REI down jacket (for layering). I think Veilance, which is the higher-end and more fashionable Arc’teryx sub-label, is a more interesting proposition. You get a sleek city cut, a nicer matte ripstop shell, and insanely warm 1000-fill down.

Rocky Mountain makes one of the sickest jackets out there. Westernwear is on a bit of a downturn—well, it’s classic, but had its cultural peak two years ago—but you are seeing this style of working Western down jacket with leather yoke pop up more and more. Rocky Mountain Featherbed is the OG, starting in the 1960s as a real brand for cattlemen and their employees. Its current iteration is actually a Japanese-owned reproduction, which means it’s the real fucking deal. It looks cool, wears hard, and is probably warmer than anything on this list.

Lululemon is based in Canada, and maybe that explains why all of its outerwear is so good. We love its bulkier puffer jacket, but for a sleeker, winter athleisure feel, this minimally puffed jacket is great, too. It keeps the heat in and water out, and with a stretchy, down-filled construction, you’re sure to stay warm and cozy.

This puffer from Aritzia was made with the same attention to detail and tech that goes into high-performance sleeping bags. It has 700-fill down, but it’s sewn with big horizontal chambers like a sleeping bag. It’ll withstand temperatures as low as -22°F, so it’s the only coat you’ll need for surviving a serious cold snap. The fit is intentionally oversized—longer in the arms and broader at the shoulders—to accommodate bulky sweaters underneath. Plus, the Japanese fabric is both water-repellent and wind-resistant.

Is this an outdoorsy jacket? No. Is it outdoors capable? Yes, absolutely. Heavy down jackets—as real mountaineers use them—are made for base camp, when you’re not moving but need a lot of heat. This is perfect for that.

The issue with most puffer jackets is that they’re not made to be especially abrasion or weather resistant. These jackets were originally made for cold and dry days on mountaintops. But Filson takes a different approach.

The Down Cruiser shell is a classic heavy oil-finished Filson cotton. That makes this jacket more weather-resistant and abrasion-resistant than any other option on this list.

So, you want the best? For performance, you can buy something cheaper from an outdoors brand. For fashion, you can get something cool and garment-dyed from Stone Island. For a merging of those sensibilities, Moncler is the only brand—the OG that made jackets for Europe’s mountaineers—that you should be buying from.

The Grenoble name denotes Moncler’s high-end ski line. Go to a nice ski resort in the Alps, and you’ll see plenty of these. Nothing looks as good or insulates as well. And if you don’t ski but want something fashion-forward, I still prefer the Grenoble line. The abrasion-resistant fabrics on the jackets look a lot better than the high-shine nylon that Moncler and other brands like to do on puffers.

While finding the perfect puffer jacket isn’t rocket science, there are a few key features you should focus on to narrow your search down from the 20 options above.

Insulation: Some climates call for the most intense down fill power there is, and some simply don’t. You don’t want to overheat this winter, nor do you want to be too cold, so go for a puffer with fill or insulation that your climate calls for. Don’t make the mistake of buying an 850-fill-power puffer if you live in a city where the coldest it ever gets is, like, 59 degrees.

Features: Not all puffer jackets are made the same. Personally, I need a removable hood. Look for design features including adjustability at the hem and hood, windproof fabric, zippered or buttoned pockets, a mock neck, and more, depending on your preferences.

Puffiness: If you’re someone who travels a lot and needs to stash your puffer in a suitcase from time to time, going for a super-puffy option probably isn’t going to be your best bet. Instead, look for puffers with a little less, well, puff, which will make them much easier to stow and pack. Some on this list even come with their own stow bags.

Being based in New York City means we know what makes a good puffer jacket. The options we listed above bring the heat in terms of functionality and style, all season long. Over the years, our editors have tried hundreds of styles and brands. Narrowing it down to our 20 favorites was no small feat, but these are just the picks that we swear by and return to time and time again for our winter wear.

Fill power is the number that tells you the quality of down in your jacket. The number technically indicates how many cubic inches of loft a single ounce of down has, but for simplicity, just focus on whether the number is relatively high or low—the higher it is, the higher-quality the insulation is: 600 is good, 700 is great, and 800 is spectacular.

Over time, as down goes through washing machines, tough weather conditions, and daily life, it can lose its fluffiness and slightly deflate your puffer. It’ll still keep you warm and dry, sure, just not quite so puffy.

#6899. How to Style Rain Boots for the Fall and Winter Months

Once reserved for muddy festival fields, countryside walks, and unpredictable weather, Wellington boots have officially stepped into the fashion spotlight and are the trending shoe I can’t get enough of. Functionality is super important for me during the fall and winter months. And there’s nothing I love more than a two-fer style piece. Rain boots are not only practical footwear for the cold and wet days ahead but they can be just as chic, too.

Forget the idea that rain boots are simply something to throw on when the forecast looks gloomy. Today’s versions are a fashion statement in their own right. As temperatures drop and rainy mornings become part of the daily routine, investing in a stylish pair of rain boots makes perfect fashion sense. After all, why let a little bad weather dampen your style? This season, embrace the puddles, forget the forecast, and step out in boots that prove rainy days can still be fashion-forward. To help you out, I’ve gathered some easy style inspo for your perusing so you can tap into this popular shoe style.

This mixture of leather jacket with Hunter boots is the perfect go-to outfit when you’re running out of the house. It is simple but allows you to still look put together.

Cropped trench coats are in, and pairing them with your favorite rain boots and a frilly, lace skirt is tapping into so many trends at one time.

For those actually gloomy days, a good, thick wool coat with your rain boots is the way to go. This outfit is also made even more fun with the lace jorts and blue hat, adding layers to the look.

While fall is still in full effect, utilizing the khaki-colored pieces in your closet is the way to go. Also, if you’re not into the knee-high rain boots, try out the ankle-length booties instead.

Whether it be a dress or a matching set, when struggling to find an outfit this fall and winter, pulling out a warm two-piece look or dress is how you’re going to get out the door quicker while still looking fashionable.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

#6898. Designers Reveal the Fastest Way They’ve Updated a Living Room Under $100

A few small swaps can make your space feel brand new.

A living room is arguably the most popular spot of the house, so why not give it the tender loving care it deserves with a design refresh? Problem is, when it comes to most home projects, it’s easy to convince yourself good things only come to those who wait—or anyone willing to fork over a small fortune.

Fortunately, affordability and efficiency are no longer mutually exclusive. According to the pros, it’s possible to elevate a space swiftly and without spending much money. Want proof? Keep scrolling for the clever (and quick) ways designers have upgraded a living room for $100 or less.

Barbra Fordyce of Ginger Lemon Indigo says plants are “hands-down” one of the best low-cost, high-impact ways to spruce up your space. “Bringing something living into your living room instantly changes the mood, quite literally bringing life, nature and an organic beauty into the room,” explains the designer. While fresh foliage can look good anywhere, she loves how plants can add a “verdant richness” to neutral rooms without throwing off the entire color palette.

That said, houseplants aren’t a one-size-fits-all situation, so choose wisely.

“If you have high ceilings, you want the plant to be proportional to those dimensions,” Fordyce says. “If the plant or tree is too small in a large living room, it will feel dwarfed.”

For the perfect finishing touch, use your leftover budget to buy a beautiful planter. “The container should echo the feeling of your room,” she shares. “A basket, ceramic container or even a vintage wooden crate can all make beautiful ‘pots’ for your plants.”

Though there is such a thing as too many throw pillows, the right ones can make a difference. “A pillow and throw refresh can go a long way in bringing new life to your sofa,” explains Natalie Kaelin, an interior designer in Raleigh, North Carolina.

Of course, there’s no need to buy brand-new lumbar and square cushions on a whim: Simply purchase covers for your existing pillows and swap them out seasonally.

Neutral linen layers can conjure coastal grandmother vibes virtually anywhere, while plush, jewel-toned velvets offer an elegant, autumnal look. Come winter, embrace the holiday spirit with flannel fabric or a plaid repeat.

We might spend a lot of time talking about layered lighting or how to pick the perfect fixture, but we cannot stress it enough: Pay attention to your bulbs.

“Daylight LED bulbs are incredibly white, so they put out light akin to doctors’ offices and hospitals,” says Elizabeth Cross-Beard of Palm Henri in Baltimore. “A warmer temperate bulb gives a home a candle-light, cozy feel.” As a general rule of thumb, she recommends buying lightbulbs with a temperature of 2700K and avoiding anything above 4000K.

Cross-Beard says switching your bulbs can effectively “redesign” your space, and it’s shockingly inexpensive. “Depending on how many lights you have, it can cost as little as $50,” she adds.

If you want to upgrade your space without spending a cent, Blair Burton says to start with your furniture.

“Sometimes simply pulling furniture away from the walls and creating a more comfortable conversation area can completely change how a room feels,” the Austin-based designer explains.

After all, just because an armchair has been in the corner of your living room for years doesn’t mean it needs to go there. By doing a room rearrangement, you might end up using it more when it’s in front of a warm, sun-drenched window. Maybe you’ll even realize you need to pick up an ottoman to create the ultimate cozy space.

Another $0 hack? Burton also encourages her clients to “shop their homes.” “It’s practical, of course, but it's also about honoring the things they've collected and loved over the years,” she explains. “Sometimes finding an unexpected new home for a favorite piece is one of the most satisfying discoveries in a project.”

“Adding artwork, whether it's a gallery wall, a single large-scale piece, or something in between, makes a huge visual and personal impact in a living room,” says Molly Torres Portnof of DATE Interiors in New York.

Retailers like Minted and Society6 offer well-priced prints, but you can also reframe photographs and posters you already own. As long as you pay attention to scale and proportion, the ceiling’s really the limit. “Rules are meant to be broken,” Portnof says. “If it feels right and makes you happy, you're on the right track.”

If your living room is blessed with beautiful built-ins, you can fill them with treasures you already own, or buy a few new pieces for well under $100.

“Take the time to create vignettes,” advises Tennessee designer Katie Kiser. “More often than not, I find my clients have all the pieces for a well-choreographed space, but they're styled improperly.”

For an instant yet affordable upgrade, head over to your local antique store to pick up some art books, jars, or a trinket tray.

#6897. Gigi Hadid’s Guest In Residence Is Taking Its Signature Knits to the Slopes With Halfdays

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like peanut butter and jelly or macaroni and cheese, cozy knits and skiwear just go together. After all, bundling up in layers is of the utmost priority when you’re hitting the slopes—and even more so when you’re taking part in some après-ski. So it only made sense that the female-founded and cool girl-approved ski brand Halfdays would team up with one of the coolest girls of our generation: Gigi Hadid—and more specifically, Hadid’s luxury knitwear brand, Guest In Residence.

“Most of my favorite winter memories are tied to being outside, getting dressed for the cold, and staying out until the last run,” Hadid shares in a press release. “With Halfdays, we wanted to bring that feeling into the Guest In Residence world, pairing our signature cozy warmth with technical pieces you actually want to wear on the mountain. The collection mixes sporty, vintage, nostalgic and playful influences, and is absolutely at home on the slopes.”

The retro-inspired collection—which is available to shop now—consists of eight pieces, including a corduroy ski bib, a coverall, a puffer jacket inspired by cable-knit sweaters, a Fair Isle turtleneck, and waffle-knit base layers. Combine with a color palette reminiscent of the ’70s alpine scene, and you have what is sure to be a statement on the slopes—and off. “As we think about how Halfdays can live beyond the slopes, knitwear felt like a natural next step,” Ariana Ferwerda, Halfdays’s co-founder and CEO, shared. “Guest In Residence brought its expertise in warmth and knitwear, while we brought our technical approach to mountain dressing. Together, we were able to create something that feels fashion-forward without compromising the performance women need on the mountain.”

Whether you’re the type who’s on the slopes until the ski lift closes or you prefer to stay at the lodge sipping on a hot toddy (or maybe you’re the girl who does both), this collection will keep you looking cute no matter how you prefer to spend your days on the mountain.

#6892. The Shattered Bob Is Fall’s Most Wearable Short Haircut

Polished yet effortless, this is a look that can be easily tailored to every hair type.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The bob is undoubtedly the haircut of the year, and it shows no sign of losing momentum as summer gives way to cooler weather. While warmer days favored sharp edges and sculpted lines, this fall, the bob is taking on a softer, more undone shape, with a relaxed, less polished finish.

Enter the shattered bob: a tousled, textured take on the classic cut that captures the undone mood seen at London Fashion Week and on celebrities including Margot Robbie, Halle Berry, and Indira Varma. Natural texture is taking center stage, bringing movement and ease to a style once defined by sculptural precision.

The shattered bob is a softer, more relaxed take on the classic cut. It looks polished without feeling overly styled. “We’re looking at jaw-to-shoulder lengths, slightly broken edges, subtle graduation, and a heavier front. It should feel lived-in rather than perfectly placed, almost as though the haircut has naturally fallen into that shape,” say hairstylists Stephen Buller and Anita Rice.

Cut to fall softly and grow out seamlessly, it needs far less day-to-day styling while still looking considered. The style reflects a wider shift in beauty toward embracing hair’s natural texture and a style that feels authentic, luxurious, and easy to manage.

Movement is key to the shattered bob. Ask your stylist for soft, varied layers and lightly broken-up ends to add texture and soften the cut’s outline. It can work especially well with wavy or curly hair, as layers enhance its natural shape and movement.

Bring reference photos of people with a similar hair type to help guide the consultation. Your stylist can use them to tailor the cut to your natural texture and face shape.

Keeping products light is the best way to maintain the cut’s airy feel. Styling should be minimal, as the appeal of the look is in its effortlessness. “There’s still that polished fashion element, but it’s being roughed up. It feels more individual and ultimately more wearable,” add Buller and Rice.

For texture and separation without weighing the hair down, work a bit of a texturizing mousse or cream like Davines Dry Texturizer through the mid-lengths and ends.

Ultimately, the shattered bob should be about ease and hair that works alongside your lifestyle, showing that the most sophisticated hair often looks effortless.

#6888. 33 Bedroom Plants That Bring a Calming Touch to Your Space

Do you have one of these in your room yet?

Want to improve your sleep naturally? Try adding a few plants to your bedroom. Beyond offering charm and a cozy atmosphere, plants have been linked to mental and physical benefits, including sleep. Research suggests that being around greenery can lift your mood, ease stress, and promote a greater sense of well-being. Bringing a touch of nature into your sleeping space may be a simple way to add those positive qualities to the room.

Plants may also help calm the mind and move the body from an alert state toward a more restful one, potentially supporting better sleep. A past study linked indoor greenery with more positive emotions and fewer negative ones, which may help you fall asleep faster. The findings point to a broader connection between greenery and well-being, including its possible role in a restful bedroom. It’s easy to see why biophilic design—an approach that evokes nature indoors—has become a favorite among designers.

Ahead, find the best plants for your bedroom. We made sure the options would thrive in all types of lights, but especially in low light, so that whether you’re a beginner or an experienced green thumb, you will find the right verdant friend to grow.

The Aspidistra elatior thrives in low to bright shade, making it perfect for bedrooms that don’t get too much direct light. A fun fact: This plant historically was referred to as the “bar room” plant or “beer plant” because it could survive smoky, low-light conditions in Victorian-era taverns and halls. It is non-toxic to humans, cats, and dogs so if you have pets this is a great option.

Native to the rainforests of Brazil, the Maranta leuconeura is now a common indoor houseplant. During the day, the oval leaves lie flat to soak up the light and at night, the leaves fold upward like praying hands, hence the name.

For a trailing plant that thrives in bright, indirect light get a wax plant. It appreciates higher humidity levels so if you live in an older home with fluctuating humidity, this is the verdant plant baby for you.

For beginner and intermediate plant owners, the nerve plant is a great option because they thrive in most indoor conditions, remain compact, and are safe for pets. They will signal to you what they need so you can water and choose the soil for it accordingly.

Pilea peperomioides, or the Chinese money plant, is a cheerful-looking, easy-to-propagate plant that is incredibly low-maintenance. It's a great plant for a sunny bedroom. All plant parents need to do is place it in bright, indirect sunlight and water only when the top inch of soil feels dry.

Aglaonema tolerates very low light levels, so it's ideal if you don't have large windows in your bedroom. Water it when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The snake plant doesn't mind low light—and because it's a succulent, it only needs to be watered every week or two.

Purported to bring good luck, the money tree has a nice, modern vibe—plus, it's not fussy and grows quickly. Give it medium light, water it every week or two, and it'll be happy.

The pothos plant tolerates any light level from low to high. (That means it's super-easy to keep alive!) Just don't water it until the top few inches of soil feel dry to the touch—overwatering is the only real way to kill this plant.

Lavender has long been considered beneficial for relaxation and sleep. Although most research has studied the use of lavender essential oils specifically, having a live lavender plant in your bedroom probably can't hurt. It needs bright light, so make sure it's on a sunny windowsill or use a grow light. Water when the top inch or two feels dry to the touch.

This handsome vining plant is a great choice for new plant parents. Its glossy, heart-shaped leaves are beautiful as they spill over the edges of nightstands or bookshelves–and it's tolerant of low light levels. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

This adorable fern is one of the best to raise indoors. Its thick, waxy leaves retain water better than many other ferns that have finer fronds—and it won't leave a mess by dropping foliage every day. Give it bright indirect light and water when the top inch of soil feels dry.

This fun plant looks like an old-fashioned ric rac trim. It will grow like crazy in bright indirect light—and because it's a succulent, it needs watering only every week to 10 days.

This social media darling is striking and easy to grow—it just needs bright indirect light. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The peace lily will adapt well to low light levels, but it needs bright indirect light to bloom. Water when the top inch or two feel dry.

This cute palm that looks just like a messy ponytail likes bright indirect light. And the bulbous base stores water, so it needs a drink only every few weeks when the soil is totally dry.

This handsome plant boasts a beautiful upright form, so it makes an eye-catching statement in the bedroom. Give it bright indirect light, and water when the top few inches of soil are dry. As an outdoor landscape plant, it will flower, though that's unusual indoors.

If you're looking for low-maintenance houseplants, this is the way to go! It will tolerate low light and is often grown in water. In water, change it weekly; in soil, water so that it stays slightly moist.

This cute tabletop palm will add loads of style to your bedroom. It prefers bright indirect light, but it will tolerate medium light better than many other types of palms. Water when it's slightly dry to the touch.

#6881. Three Prada Obsessives Unpack Miuccia’s Defining Decade

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Bazaar’s Lynette Nylander and Camille Freestone join Laia Garcia-Furtado for a roundtable on her new book, Prada in the Nineties

Prada is as much an adjective as it is a noun. My colleagues and I often describe wardrobe twists, like a bejeweled brooch on a Peter Pan collar or an embroidered sock peeking out of a Mary Jane, as “very Prada.” But what is it about these little notes of character that signals “Prada” in our brain?

That’s one of the questions Bazaar’s deputy culture director, Laia Garcia-Furtado, answers in her new book, Prada in the Nineties. (Published by Batsford, it drops today.) Her deep dive into one of the brand’s most pivotal decades reaches far beyond the heroin-chic label we tend to slap on ’90s fashion. From runway reviews to cultural anecdotes, Garcia-Furtado takes us backstage, into the life and personality of Miuccia Prada, the industry’s north star of intellectual fashion.

Mrs. Prada’s arsenal of off-color prints and skirts have inspired generations of women to aspire to an ugly-chic kind of cool; looking at her today, it’s easy to find ourselves lost in her world. But in harkening back to the nascent stages of her career as a designer, Garcia-Furtado reminds us that the throughline of her genius lies in her sense of self.

It’s a story that began with nylon, an unexpected choice for a member of the Prada family, with its legacy of selling bespoke leather goods to royal clientele. Yet Mrs. Prada’s choice to bring nylon totes and duffels to the luxury market was not merely driven by rebellion—it was a manifestation of her own desire for a well-designed bag that embraced utility. And the groundbreaking success of the bags demonstrated that her dreams were shared by other women—a hint of where Prada would take the brand.

Garcia-Furtado understands this lore firsthand. After all, she’s spent a lot of time in Mrs. Prada’s shoes—well, the ones she designed, at least. She began building her own archive of Miuccia merch in college with a pair of woven leather Mary Janes by Prada’s little sister, Miu Miu. A lucky eBay find from the spring 2001 collection, they were marked by interlaced checks of leather with pops of plum purple and canvas buckle straps. She has continued to build her collection ever since.

Writing the book gave Garcia-Furtado carte blanche to dig into the nitty-gritty details that unite women of varying tastes and lifestyles as proud members of Prada’s hive. Below, she gathers with Bazaar’s fellow Prada obsessees, executive digital director Lynette Nylander and senior fashion writer Camille Freestone, to unpack all the things that have placed Mrs. Prada in a league of her own, from the ’90s to now.

Jennifer Jenkins: What inspired you to go on such a deep dive on Miuccia Prada?

Laia Garcia-Furtado: There are very specific images that we go to when we think of Prada in the ’90s, but all of those collections are from the late ’90s. So I thought, "Wait, what about the early '90s?" When I started looking up those collections, I realized that what I was really doing was tracing how Prada went from almost an independent, fledgling designer to an icon in a period of ten years, which is really crazy. I love to go down a research hole; that's actually my favorite thing. It was just great to have an excuse.

Lynette Nylander: One of the things that struck me about going through Laia’s book, and my own personal understanding of Prada in the '90s, is that it was really foundational. A lot of the signatures and markers of what Prada is today were made in that formative time. One of my favorite things about being part of the Prada hive—and I think we all identify as that—is that she has carefully constructed a brand where you can mine from its past. She's very okay with the idea that something is a rehashed idea from a '90s collection in the 2000s, in the 2010s, in the 2020s. Everything is meant to be in dialogue with everything else. That's really refreshing in a world where designers are making very distinct collections and basically almost asking you to do away with whatever you bought last year and buy into what's “now.” She very much respects the past, which is why I think a book on the '90s is brilliant.

Camille Freestone: Also, the designs are so pared back. We think now of much more heavy-handed embellishment when we imagine Prada. Part of it was that it was the '90s and it was just a simpler time. Runways have just gotten more megawatt altogether. But the Prada collections are quite pure. The design is quite simple in the best way, so they let her creative touches shine in such a way that feels very present.

LGF: I mean everything old is new, et cetera. Going back to what you were saying about the simple shapes, even when things are heavily embellished and there's a lot of beading and sequins, it never feels like putting on a bunch of stuff for the sake of putting on a bunch of stuff. Even if it's super over-the-top, you think, “It's exactly what it needs to be.” It feels very real. One of my favorite things I read while I was doing research is a quote where she's saying how in movies and in art, you're allowed to bring in real life and it's allowed to be ugly and regular, but fashion was a place where you weren't allowed to do that, and she wanted to change that. When I read that, everything from those old collections made so much sense.

But even now, I think you can read a collection, even if she and Raf are saying that it's about one thing, and you look at it, you're like, "It's really about something else."

LN: Yeah, and that's always been a part of that duality. And I mean, even in the inception of Miu Miu, she talks about it as this other side of her personality. She's someone who is really in dialogue with her more fantastical, imagined self. And I think what was interesting about going through the '90s collections was that it was fantastical, but again, more real.

But I want to talk about what you learned. They always say hindsight is 20/20, but I don't think we were lucky enough to understand what a unique time the ‘90s were. It was pre-social-media. Celebrity culture had hit this apex, and Miuccia was there as this brand that carried and collected a lot of celebrity in a way that is not how it is now.

LGF: It wasn’t grody! And I think that was especially evident when she launched menswear, and John Malkovich was her model or Willem Dafoe—I mean, now we all love Willem Dafoe, of course, but it was very different from what everyone else was doing.

And another thing that's really interesting is that we have a vision of the ’90s, but the first couple of years of the ‘90s are still the ’80s. It takes years for the prevailing aesthetic that we all remember to rise up. Going back, Prada was a heritage label, but she launched ready-to-wear in the late ‘80s. And it wasn't like when Vuitton launched ready-to-wear, when everybody understood it as a major brand. Prada was by no means in its flop era, but it wasn’t as well known as Vuitton. It was not the same.

So when she started, critics were writing things like “Prada also showed a collection.” There wasn't even a long review. And then she sort of started getting talked about in the same breath as Martin Margiela or Ann Demeulemeester, like, “These people are the plucky new designers.” We think she's so untouchable now and it's like she almost can do no wrong, but you can go back and see how it all started and see her be like, "I hated this collection actually, and I'll never do that again.” I mean, my kingdom for any designer that would say that now—to just be that honest.

JJ: You touched a lot on her unapologetic way of expressing herself, even while taking in different cultural references and touchpoints. Was there anything in particular you were most surprised to learn about her?

LGF: My favorite trivia is that she got married on Valentine's Day.

LGF: She's a romantic. I feel like that says so much.

CF: It's amazing. She was in the Communist Party of Italy, and so not of the fashion world at all, but so endlessly fascinated by it.

LGF: It's so funny because it's very much like her parents said, "Okay, you've had your fun. Now, come back home, join the family business.” And she wanted to be an actor—the mime thing! In one interview, she said, "My favorite part was when we had to do really specific body control stuff." And you can totally see her just being so good at that.

LN: Well, I think that she's so good at it because I can't think of a designer, at least in more modern history, that really sort of mines from herself. Have you ever noticed, if you read a part of a review, or you’ve seen those videos of her backstage, it's never like, "I was looking at the art of Georgia O’Keeffe”?

LN: She doesn't even mine it from a place. She isn't even saying, "Oh, I was walking down the piazza in Milan." It's not about that—it's this higher sense of self. I think about the fall/winter 2026 collection, with Bella Hadid and the four rounds of looks. And of course she did that because she's the only person who would do that, because those are the things that ticktock around in the caverns of her mind when we're all fast asleep.

CF: Well, she's oddly practical. She's kind of utilitarian. And I think now I'm just saying a different thing in different words, but you think of a crystal embellishment when you think of Prada, you think of a quirk, but it's all rooted in a really utilitarian approach. I feel like this is clothing, not art.

LGF: It's also because that's how she dresses. I mean, yes, are her high heels too high for us to walk and not get blisters? Yes, but we still wear them. It's fine. But she designs clothes that she wants to wear, and we always look at what she's wearing at the end of the show to see how she's styling.

LN: She's the model that we're looking for at the end of the show.

CF: Then it's funny to put her back in dialogue with a Martin Margiela; they both were subversive in their own ways, but hers was lensed through an idea that these are just clothes. You need to get dressed in them!

LGF: There is something about the two of them. Margiela never wanted to be interviewed, but when you look back at a lot of the profiles of her, there will be editorials where it's designers with their muses, and she's almost always by herself. She's her own muse.

It's just her. It's like her looking in a mirror. It's her and her son, or it's her by herself, but it's such a clear vision. And I mean, I think it also makes a difference because a lot of the male designers need a muse… but for her, it's always her.

CF: I'm also always fascinated by the “designer uniform.” There are so many examples. Phoebe Philo, Michael Kors, Jonathan Anderson: everyone takes their bow, and it's almost like they're the antithesis of their clothes. So obviously, we are always talking about a woman designing for women, but in that particular context of those big houses, she's one of the few who is just dressing just like her collection.

LGF: And it's amazing the few times she's come out wearing pants.

LGF: It's so rare! You're like, "Oh my god, the slim cigarette.”

LN: I have two things I want to say to the idea that she doesn't really have a muse. The muse is herself, but somehow that doesn't come across as arrogant. It's not the opposite. It comes across as, “I just do what I know I’ll respond to and hopefully other people like it.” And I think there's something really earnest about it.

CF: There's also no ideal of the perfect woman, either.

LN: Yes, in fact, I don't think she even really deals or trades in ideals.

I also want to talk about the colors. I mean, if I think about Prada, I just think about this kaleidoscopic universe of color. She can go anywhere. She can put any color combination together. It's kind of genius. But particularly in the '90s… they're all quite muted. You have this line from Robin Givhan in the book, “The greens hovered somewhere between shades of slime and mold. The browns were”—I mean, this is the beauty of the Robin Givhan written line—“the browns were murky, the color of water as it stagnates over a long, steamy summer. The dizzy and crisscross patterns were reminiscent of UHF static or an old-fashioned labyrinth in rusted plumbing.”

LGF: It's funny though because she hated all of those collections.

LN: I think if there was ever going to be a brand that's going to respect the fact that Robin Givhan ripped her to shreds, it's going to be her. I think she believes in the idea of free thought, and that was Givhan’s thought and put so beautifully.

But I do want to talk about the colors, because I just think it's so specific of how I think about Prada, but also how I think about my own relationship to color. I don't know if you feel like that, but probably in an inadvertent way, she has given me the confidence and the permission to put together these really disparate color combinations.

LGF: I mean, I think that collection that Robin is talking about, from spring ’96 (which is the root cause of my everything; I had a slime green room—I love slime green) is really interesting. Even before she got into the cool color combinations that we know her from, those early '90s collections, even when it was like, “Here's a bunch of beautiful white dresses,” suddenly there would be weird goldenrod silk and a weird orange. The color thing was always there from the beginning. And when you look at the collections throughout the years, you see how she gets a little bit more confident in that and starts mixing it. But that punch of weirdness was present from the beginning.

CF: But somehow, I think, "Why do I like this slime shade? Why do I like this brown? Why do I like this purple?" But all of a sudden, she's made me want purple.

LN: I hate purple in any other context than hers—I need purple.

CF: All of a sudden I want purple. And there's these weird plaids that all of a sudden make me think, "Oh, I need that."

LGF: Also, in the late '90s, she does collections with things embellished with mirrors, and she was like, "Well, everyone is being a hippie and doing this, so I also did it,” but she did it extra crazy. And I love when she is engaging.

Going back to what you said about her being probably one of the few designers that is like, "This review panned me, it's fine,” I feel like she really was engaging with what was being written about her.

One season editors were like, "There are no suits. This is outrageous! Where are all the suits?" So then the next season she opened the collection with a suit and it's very like, "You're welcome. Are you happy now?” I like to see her engage, not that she doesn't do that anymore, but I think in the ’90s, it was a little bit more obvious.

LN: And now she kind of hovers above it all. I want to talk about 1998. Her ascent was complete, you say, in 1998, when she was now at over a half a billion dollars in sales. She landed at 15 in Vogue’s list of the most influential brands. Other women at the top 20 were Donna Karan, Angela Missoni, and Krizia’s Mariuccia Mandelli, and Miuccia is one of the only working designers left. What was happening in 1998, and what collection is that?

LGF: It's the one with the cool red-and-white boots that are space-age. It's all very space-age mod. ‘98 is the time when she went all-in on the millennial aesthetic that everyone was into, when she did her sports thing.

CF: I also think she doesn't deal very much in volume. That's not really her game.

LN: Thank god. I don't know if I'd want a poofy Prada.

LGF: Yeah. But it's so crazy—in a decade you go from reviews that say, "Oh, Prada also showed a collection," to, "Everyone is focused on whatever she does."

LGF: I think she just became more confident in her vision, which was different from everyone else. And I mean, Tom Ford was also doing something at Gucci; there was that vibe shift, and she was part of it, but it was different. From the beginning, she was always the thinking woman's sex-appeal designer, and that just stayed. She was very in tune with the culture, but she wasn't just doing reproductions of what you could see. She was injecting it with her own aesthetic.

You can look at something and say, "Oh, this was her way of doing grunge," but it was not grunge. It was her own thing.

LN: What was that thing? I mean, do you look at broader context and get a sense of the music she listens to or the boys she kissed or the movies she liked?

LGF: Yeah, I think it's very much a movies thing. I feel like you get a sense of her upbringing. The Mary Jane—the shoes she wore as a little girl—were always a thing. She got married in a dress and a coat made by a childrenswear designer. The little socks—you start seeing those reflections of her life, and they're more apparent the more that you know her, but I don't think it's the kind of thing where you need to know what it is in order to like it. And I think there is something about it where it's for grown women. Young people also like it, but her clothes are not clothes for young teen girls.

LN: Was that also something you learned from your research?

LGF: I mean, technically, I think Miu Miu was supposed to be the younger brand, and the vibe did feel a little bit younger, but Prada always felt a little bit adult. Even when you look at the suits or the proper little silk dresses, you're like, "These are clothes for adults."

CF: I feel like that's also where the styling sneaks in. You’ll see the childlike Mary Jane with the suit and maybe one little brooch or something that is so subtle…but it's not hitting you over the head.

LGF: Yeah, because I think there's also a thing where you always look up to something. You know how you read Seventeen magazine when you're 12? And I think there is something about Prada where no matter how old you are, it still feels just beyond where you currently are.

LN: Every time I buy a piece, it still feels, in a weird way, unattainable, or I question if I'm mature or smart or cool enough.

LGF: And then you look in the mirror, and it feels like it feels exactly where you should be.

CF: It is like, "Am I a woman? Am I a glamorous woman?"

LN: Yeah. Maybe it's just because we think about her so much as the likeness of that.

CF: Well, we have made her the ideal almost. She is the one to be judged against, for so many people, but then that makes it hard to articulate what she actually is.

LGF: But even her glamour—there is something attainable about it. She doesn't wear crazy makeup, and she doesn't style her hair in any kind of way. It's just her holding her little scarf, and you think, "I need a scarf to hold!" Going back to her interest in real life, it's just so her. And who doesn't want to just be like themselves? You want to be her, but you really just want to be you.

#6878. Honestly, I Wouldn’t Risk Waiting for Black Friday—Grab the Oura Ring 4 for $150 Off While Your Size Is Still Here

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

It’s never a bad time to invest in your health, but right now is a particularly good one. While Black Friday is still a couple of weeks away, the Oura Ring 4 (the predecessor to the Oura Ring 5, which came out in June) is getting the early sale treatment. And trust me when I say this isn’t one of those wellness gadgets that’s all hype and no substance. Just ask, ahem, the entire Cosmo shopping team. This little wearable tech tracker—which is over $100 off on Amazon—does it all.

Now, I know what you’re thinking: Why not wait until November 27 and snag one with the rest of your Black Friday haul? Well, while we don’t know exactly what Oura has up its sleeve for the Super Bowl of shopping, there’s no guarantee today’s deal will stick around, that it’ll get any better, or that your size will even be in stock by the main event. Waiting is just a gamble, and hey, a good deal is a good deal! If you’ve been waiting to buy one and don’t need the newest model, I’d take the $249 deal and run! Brand-new models don’t usually see big discounts their first holiday season, so I wouldn’t count on the Oura Ring 5 getting much cheaper this year.

For what it’s worth, the Oura Ring 5 is about 40% smaller and gets up to nine days of battery life, compared to the Ring 4’s eight. But many of its new app features are rolling out to older models too, so unless a slimmer ring is a priority, the Ring 4 still does the job. The Oura Ring 4 is sleek, lightweight, and pairs with the rest of your jewelry so effortlessly—whether you’re a gold or silver girly, it’ll stack gorgeously. There are six different colorways, which vary slightly in price: Silver, Black, and Stealth are $249 (previously $349), and Gold and Rose Gold are $279 (originally $399). The ceramic design in midnight is even marked down to $279 from $399 today.

But it’s so much more than just a style choice. The device keeps tabs on your steps, activity, sleep, stress levels, heart rate, temperature, and cycle, and it keeps all that trackable data in a convenient mobile app.

One shopper called it a “game-changer” as someone living with chronic illnesses and said they’re able to “better understand [their] body, track [their] patterns, and recognize changes.” Another added: “As a woman, the cycle tracking alone has been incredibly valuable. Being able to see how different phases of my cycle affect my sleep, recovery, stress levels, energy, and overall readiness has helped me make much better decisions about work, exercise, and self-care.” That’s a pretty compelling argument alone!

#6876. Certifiably Chic Outfits to Wear to Thanksgiving Dinner

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

We know we’ve been talking about festive party attire, but we may have gotten a little ahead of ourselves. #6875. Nina Katchadourian Spent 11 Years Making Art on Airplanes

The hundreds of photographs are gathered in a new book, Seat Assignment

There are few places where the unwieldiness and malleability of time take center stage quite like when one is on an airplane. It is one of the few times when the world allows for a kind of self-indulgence: You can sleep, catch up on your favorite TV shows, watch a guilty pleasure movie, read a book, or listen to a podcast that you never had time for while on the ground, or you could even raw-dog the flight, just you and your thoughts, getting lost as you hurl through the air at 580 miles per hour inside a hunk of aluminum and other composite materials.

In 2010, the artist Nina Katchadourian was sitting on a plane at LaGuardia Airport in New York on her way to Atlanta, begrudging all of the frequent flying that was keeping her away from her studio and her work. “I thought, ‘Here I am again, about to spend three hours flying somewhere, and it feels as if this time already doesn’t count,’ ” she recalls thinking. “It’s garbage time.” But Katchadourian, whose interdisciplinary approach has led her to work with a wide array of mediums and materials (photography, performance, sculpture, “audio interventions,” a popcorn machine), called herself out. “I thought [it was] a really bad attitude for somebody like me, who claims as an artist to be interested in mundane things and everyday things and believes that things often deserve a closer look.” She challenged herself to “make things the whole way to Atlanta” with whatever she could find around her, photographed with her iPhone. The first “improvisations,” as she called them, included classic airplane objets like pretzels (see them resting comfortably on a king bed), straws (turned into bridges over a mountainscape, and the shiny buckle on the seatbelt (a perfect medium for a self-portrait). “By the time I got to Atlanta, I thought, ‘I need to keep doing this because something just happened and I need to see where it will go.’ ” She’d work on it until 2021, when the pandemic provided a natural pause.

Now the entire project has been published as a small tome appropriately titled Seat Assignment. The hundreds of images are a testament to how curiosity about the world can open and create an alternate universe that lives exactly within the limits of our own, and Katchadourian is open to both the humorous (a little puppet face made from a bread roll with blueberries for eyes and a strawberry tongue, politicians and businessmen with noses and beards made out of pieces of croissants and radishes, a pile of sweater that resembles a gorilla) and the sublime (a Stonehenge-esque architectural formation made from mint Life Savers, mountains dotted with sweater-fuzz black clouds, mysterious trees with twisted-napkin vines that wrap around them). On a particularly long flight from San Francisco to Auckland, New Zealand, she holed up in the lavatory and produced a series of portraits “in the Flemish style,” repurposing toilet-seat covers and paper towels into dramatic collars and hats. The portraits are uncanny, often capturing the masters’ chiaroscuro effect.

She quickly set some ground rules as she embarked on the project: She wouldn’t bring anything to “make” art on the plane, could only work with what she found, and would only use her iPhone to document what she’d made. “I felt like doing [those things] would make everything too easy, and it would make it all too much like ‘art,’ ” she explains. Curiously, during the years she worked on Seat Assignment, the quality of the camera on the iPhone improved so much that it being part of her rules became less important. “In those early days, people would say to me, ‘Well, why aren’t you using a real camera?’ ” she explains. “That question disappeared after a while.”

In a way, it seems like it was only a matter of time before Katchadourian embarked on an airplane project. As a child, she traveled every summer with her parents and younger brother from Northern California, where she grew up, to a small island off the south coast of Finland, where her mother’s family was from. The island was hugely influential on how she saw the world (“I’ve made a lot of artwork there, and I really feel like my approaches to artmaking have been learned there”), but so was the long flight itself. She’d make up games with her brother, trying to guess the movie that would play during the flight (back in the day when there was only one television screen for passengers to watch). On one occasion, her mother brought sheets and blankets and made a bed and pillows for the two young children on the floor beneath their seats. “They would never let you do this now, but I remember when it was my turn to sleep down there, looking to the side and seeing this landscape of legs that went through the whole plane. It was amazing, an underworld of under the seats.” She adds, “The whole plane was a place that we were all temporary residents of, and it wasn’t a place I disliked at all. It was this space that took us from one place I loved to another place I loved.”

This point of view has guided much of her work, like “Sorted Books,” another ongoing project she started in 1993, where she organizes book collections—whether belonging to a friend or an institution—into poems or literary fragments; “The Genealogy of the Supermarket,” which began in 2005, where she organizes all of the “people” that feature in grocery products (Betty Crocker, Orville Redenbacher, the Gerber Baby) into a genealogical tree; and “Mended Spiderwebs,” where she completed spiderwebs with delicate red thread. Though disparate, her projects always invite audiences to truly look at the world around them. “I think for the first 10 years it used to concern me a lot that I never did the same things two times in a row,” Katchadourian says. “After a while, I realized that’s actually a methodology. It’s not a deficit; it’s a way of working. A way of saying the medium is secondary to the approach.”

“I think a lot of projects for me have come out of a ‘what if?’ sort of question that I ask myself, and then I try to sort out an inquiry,” she says. Curiosity and happenstance, or the idea of chance, are essential in Katchadourian’s practice. “The weird thing about research is that you don’t always know that you’re doing it, so sometimes you’re reading or having experiences and only later do you realize, ‘Oh, all that curiosity was actually in service of trying to figure this thing out.’ ” She continues, “I think you have to take diversions seriously because they are showing you something back if you’re willing to observe yourself doing it. There’s something in it that often holds a lot of information.” It’s also what her work does.

#6870. Madewell and The Six Bells Bring Storybook Charm to the Holidays

The Hudson Valley inn lends its old-world touch to the brand.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There’s nothing quite like the holiday season to boost your sense of nostalgia for cozy moments at home—especially when they involve a touch of cold-weather whimsy. Whether you’re sipping on a hot coffee or tea as you watch the changing leaves or you’re curled up next to a warm fireplace with your latest read, the colder months of the year are primed for ample charm. Of course, these moments are not without their accoutrements, which is right where the new Madewell collaboration with The Six Bells—a country inn located in the Hudson Valley—comes in.

The Six Bells Inn, founded by Audrey Gelman, features interiors inspired by the imagined English village known as Barrow’s Green—complete with its own fictional characters and landmarks—giving the space an old-world touch that feels as if it’s been plucked right out of a storybook. It’s this distinct aesthetic, along with Gelman’s way of storytelling, that led to the inn’s collaboration with Madewell.

“Steven [Cateron, Madewell’s creative director,] and the team at Madewell understood our little world at Six Bells from the very first conversation we had. In the early stages of working together on this collaboration, we wanted this collection to feel like stepping through a doorway into another time where things feel a little more enchanted,” Gelman told ELLE.com in an email. “Every piece in the collection was designed to have that sense of wonder. We hope it brings the spirit of The Six Bells into people’s lives and homes this holiday season.”

With a range of apparel, accessories, and home décor—including vintage-inspired needlepoint ornaments and pillows, stockings, and toiletry bags featuring custom prints based on the inn’s interiors—the collection combines Madewell’s laid-back style with The Six Bells’s country charm. “For the first time, we’re really leaning into home accents, which let us extend [the Madewell] world beyond what you wear and into the spaces you live in,” Cateron said in an email. “The inn’s wallpapers and patterns were a huge source of inspiration, and you’ll find bits of them throughout the collection, right down to the lining of our Denim Trucker Jacket.” There are also pajama sets and even a nightgown for those who want to take one of summer’s biggest trends straight into the holiday season. And with prices ranging from $17 to $248, there are plenty of pieces ready for the gift-giving season (and no doubt, whoever receives these will be tickled to unwrap them).

The limited-edition, 23-piece collection officially launches on November 12 at 10 a.m.; however, ELLE.com readers have exclusive access to the collection now. Shop the collection below.

#6867. I Tried Taylor Swift’s Posture-Correcting Sports Bra—and I Have Some Thoughts

Is the $218 price tag worth it? Here’s the verdict.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

I have clocked a handful of years as a shopping editor, which means that my eye is trained to see things other people might miss. For example, when we were all oohing and ahhing over Taylor Swift’s TIME Person of the Year profile, I noticed that TayTay was wearing an interesting sports bra in one of her Eras Tour dance rehearsal photos. I did some digging, and it turns out that she was rocking the Forme Power Bra, a posture-correcting bra designed to create proper alignment in your shoulders and upper back. Fancy, fancy!

Considering how grueling Taylor’s Eras Tour rehearsals were (“Every day I would run on the treadmill, singing the entire set list out loud,” she told TIME), having supportive gear designed by an orthopedic surgeon makes all the sense in the world.

And while my days consist more of imagining I’m performing “Cruel Summer” while Hot Girl Walking on the treadmill than actually doing stadium tours, I immediately thought to myself that the Forme Power Bra might just be the posture solution I needed. Like, why yes, I’d love to sit less like a shrimp and more like a 5’11” pop star engaged to an NFL player. So I, along with a couple of fellow Cosmo editors, tested it out—because if I can't have an eight-carat diamond engagement ring (or a wedding at Madison Square Garden), I'll take this instead.

Keep reading to get the real scoop on whether Taylor’s posture-correcting sports bra is worth the $$$—and which version to snag—below.

If the phrase “posture-correcting device” conjures up images of a scary, hardware-filled contraption, think again. The sports bra is less brace-like and more supportive, using fabrics of various tensions throughout the design to keep your shoulders back and gently guide your body into correct alignment whether you’re standing up, sitting down, or exercising.

The Power Bra comes in a range of colors in sizes XS to 3XL. I’ll admit it’s not the most affordable thing out there at $188. But it’s definitely fancier than your average sports bra thanks to all the FDA-registered technology—you can even spend your pre-tax HSA money on it if you want!

When I first reviewed this bra a couple of years ago, my biggest wish was that it had some sort of inner shelf bra lining or padding. And in 2025, the brand fixed that!

Forme debuted an upgraded Power+ Bra last year, a premium style that features discreet, removable padding and a wider underarm opening to allow for more mobility. It now comes in seven colors and has a slightly higher price tag at $218.

For someone like me who prefers a little nip coverage, it's totally worth opting for this slightly-upgraded option. I know myself, and I'm just way more likely to wear this one to the gym or under clothes because it functions more like a sports bra or regular bra thanks to the padding.

To illustrate my commitment to testing all the things that Blondie owns, I will tell you all that I did, in fact, try on the sports bra in a very tiny office bathroom stall (pls see photo evidence below). And not going to lie, it was a little tricky to put on at first. But don't let that scare you! It was easier to put on the second time, and even easier the third time, and the fourth time, and so on. The brand recommends stretching the armhole a few times before wearing it, which I found to be helpful.

My initial reaction was that the Forme bra provided instant relief from my slouch-induced shoulder and neck pain. It really does help you be more mindful of your posture. I even tried to curve my shoulders forward at one point, and wildly enough, realized it was way harder than normal to slouch my shoulders into Gremlin Mode. It acted as a gentle reminder that the most comfortable way to sit is with my shoulders down and back.

Also, it’s worth noting that I have a mild case of scoliosis, and I was worried that the bra would feel like a brace because the natural resting state of my spine is a liiiiittle bit off center. But I didn’t run into any issues—it even supported my one shoulder that has a tendency to pop out!

As far as sizing, Cosmo’s Deputy Shopping Director Kim Duong and I tried on our regular sizes, and we both found the sports bra to be pretty dang tight. It’s very compressive and lacks cups—this led to flat-from-the-side, noticeable-from-the-front Regency-style cleave à la Bridgerton, so I’d suggest sizing up if you have a large chest. Kim and I agreed at the time that the Power Bra, which is made of a pretty thin, nippy athletic material, would be vastly improved with the addition of removable cups or inserts—and in August 2025, the brand heard our prayers with the Power+ Bra that features removable padding!

Beauty Editor Beth Gillette also tested it out: "I was hesitant—I'd read reviews from our team but hadn't seen anyone in my size (14, DD chest) rave about it. But I might actually love it more than Kim and Hannah! It's very comfortable, shockingly, considering I have horrendous posture, according to my chiropractor. It pulls back my shoulders a bit without pinching anything and doesn't feel unnatural, either," Gillette raved.

"And I love how good it looks," she continued. "I am not particularly ~gifted~ in the ~perky~ boob department. I often describe my boobs as living permanently in my armpits. But! This lifts and shapes them rather nicely. I felt v confident walking around in it and even felt compelled to take some selfies, which is how you know."

I enjoyed the Power Bra the most while wearing it on my busiest, most active days. I’m talkin’ workout classes, running to catch the bus, and hauling boxes out of your friend’s apartment—on those days, the garment felt less tight and more supportive in the same way that a good pair of compression leggings or a high-impact sports bra helps with your performance.

However, I struggled to wear it for long periods of time on work days spent typing away at my desk, especially when I first started wearing the Power Bra. I was always dyingggg to take the sports bra off by the end of the day. My body would be tired of being reminded to sit perfectly straight for hours, and it felt great to rip off the posture-correcting garment.

But after getting used to the sports bra over the course of more than a year, I actually feel relief putting the Power Bra on after a day of slouching. It's a way of reminding my body that it ultimately feels the best when it's aligned correctly rather than automatically slumping into a weird, compressed curve. But it's definitely something that takes practiced stamina to wear for long periods of time, so don't give up!

Kim had a similar experience: “It’s not the miracle bra one may hope it is. It definitely requires some active willpower to transform your slouching habits,” she explained. “Overall, I think it would be a good bra to wear while you’re running errands or rehearsing for a worldwide tour, but I found it to unfortunately be too uncomfy for me to wear at my desk.”

Beth, on the other hand, totally fell in love with the bra—especially for workouts. "My Pilates instructor actually kept complimenting my form(e, hehe), and even kept nudging me to add heavier resistance and to do more intense poses," she noted. "In her words, I was 'a pro." And I'll be totally honest, no instructor has ever, ever, ever said that to me, so I have to believe that some of it is because of this bra."

And as far as results? While wearing the Power Bra would give me an instant posture boost, I honestly haven't seen a noticeable, lasting difference in my posture despite “training” it by wearing the Power Bra under my clothes. When I took it off, I felt like I sagged right back into my slight but regular slouch. All of the posture magic seems to happen while you’re wearing the Power Bra, which is why I’d recommend it as extra support during a yoga class, dance rehearsals, or a super active day… not as something to wear Every! Single! Day! in hopes of magically curing your posture.

Taylor's sports bra comes in a bunch of colors...

While the Power Bra didn't transform me into a billionaire with an NFL husband (🥲), I totally understand why Taylor invested in this product for her dance rehearsals. The Forme bra (with stretchier fabric in the front and tighter fabric in the back to align your shoulders properly!) is a great way to remember to mind your posture when you’re being active, especially for shorter periods of time when you can tolerate the tight compression of the fabric.

And although I haven't seen any mind-blowing, permanent results over more than a year of testing, anything that reminds me what correct alignment should feel like (for both aesthetic and general health purposes) feels like a win in the long run. It was super easy to wear under clothes, so while it’s probably not going to be an everyday bra for me, I’ll definitely be taking advantage of it for Pilates classes, hiking, or more physically taxing days.

Next step? Learning Taylor’s Vigilante Sh*t choreo...

Hannah is the Shopping Editor at Cosmopolitan, covering all things from chic home decor to trendy fashion finds, TikTok products that are actually worth your $$$, and the perfect gift to buy for your boyfriend's mom. She previously wrote for Seventeen and CR Fashion Book. Follow her on Instagram for hot takes on red carpet fashion and pictures of her office outfits that nobody asked for.

#6864. How Marty Supreme Costume Designer Miyako Bellizzi Really Shops

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The consummate downtown girl joins Bazaar’s podcast to talk about collecting vintage skirt suits and Supreme hoodies

The Good Buy is a podcast from Harper’s Bazaar in which editors Leah Chernikoff and Lynette Nylander invite celebrities, designers, models, and tastemakers to talk shop: what they buy, where they got it, and why it matters. Come down the fashion rabbit hole and take a peek inside the closets and shopping carts of the world’s most stylish people. Learn how they use style to tell their stories.

From Uncut Gems to Marty Supreme, Oscar-nominated costume designer Miyako Bellizzi is responsible for dressing some of the most memorable films of the past decade. A longtime collaborator with the Safdie brothers—Joshua and Benjamin—she’s brought the filmmakers’ stories to life with detail-oriented styling that feels lived-in and real. Whether capturing the ostentatious wardrobe of a 2012 Diamond District hustler or crafting 1950s-accurate ensembles for over 3,000 background actors in the Timothée Chalamet–led ping-pong drama, she’s proven time and time again she has a knack for storytelling.

In her personal life, this expertise has translated into an eclectic closet full of vintage finds. Known for mixing high-end designer pieces with streetwear pulls and 1940s workwear, she dresses herself with as much intention as she does the characters you see on screen. Showing up to film this week’s installment of Harper’s Bazaar’s The Good Buy, she paired a fitted blazer from her collection of 1940s and ’50s skirt suits with shorts she made herself, Phoebe Philo flats, and sculptural silver statement earrings.

“I love costume design, I love fashion, I love film, I love it all,” she said. “I love people, so I think that’s why.”

In the episode, Bellizzi dives into everything from costuming the screen to shopping for herself. Read on for highlights and watch the show in full above.

“I’ve always loved vintage, I think my parents put me onto it. My grandma had all these clothes from New York from the ’50s and ’60s that she didn’t want, and my dad is a big vintage buyer. Even my Barbies were vintage Barbies. But I also just appreciate well-made pieces, I appreciate good fabrics, things that you can’t find today. As a designer, it’s fascinating to see how things were made back in different eras.

“I collect vintage 1940s and ’50s skirt suits. It’s always been my favorite era, which was why Marty Supreme was a dream film for me. I think I started collecting in high school—we used to have vintage fairs in San Francisco, where I’m from. I like the shape and silhouette. This is what Mugler referenced. I’m a historical person and I love the shapes and silhouettes of Mugler, and I have a lot of Mugler, but also I know where it comes from, so I like to go to the source.”

“I don’t personally buy a lot of contemporary brands for myself, other than Phoebe Philo and the Row. I [shop there for] very classic basics, because I find all the freaky weird things vintage.”

“When I was a kid, I was like 5’9” in the fifth grade. I was really tall. And we’d always have to shop in the boy’s section. When I first started, I was at Details Magazine. It was my first internship; that’s why I moved to New York. But I always felt out of place. I never felt like I fit in with the fashion girlies of that time. And so I remember enjoying menswear and learning about menswear and tailoring, and I found that was more inspiring to me than being in the women’s fashion closet.”

“I was at Vice and I was doing all the fashion stories, and there was a mass exodus and it was the group that left that asked if I would be interested in doing a film. I had never done one before, and I was like, ‘Sure, I’ll try.’ Then, [with the Safdie brothers,] we had mutual friends. The film industry is very small. Everything is timing. I never thought I wanted to be a costume designer; this was not the goal when I first moved to New York. But I love it because it’s this family—you travel with your crew and you’re doing these jobs together and you live together. You’re living and breathing this alternate reality. All you’re thinking about are these characters. It’s almost like how an actor has to get into character. You have to become the character you’re creating.”

“It was tough, honestly. You have to think about yourself being presented in that way. Traditionally, most costume designers aren’t fashion girlies. I was excited for it but it was also very stressful. I remember I was working in Paris at the time and I was staying with [designer] Gabe [Semeraro]. I was freaking out. I went to a few vintage archives there, being like, ‘At least I’m in Paris. I can find a dress.’ And I did find a really beautiful red Galliano dress that I loved that I was going to rent, but I was like, ‘I don’t know, it’s so expensive.’ One night, I was home with Gabe, and he was like, ‘Babe, I’m just gonna make one for you.’ It was one of my favorite dresses I’ve ever worn or seen.”

“Every time I wrap a film, it feels like an achievement. I remember I was doing a film in Hawaii, and when I was there, I loved that the women wore their wealth on their wrists. They stack solid gold bangles [on their wrists], and most of times they have names of people in their families [engraved]. And they’re the most beautiful things. I figured out where to find them—at a flea market—and I got one and had it engraved. Now I’ve turned it into a thing where I buy myself a piece of jewelry or something really nice after I wrap a film."

“I am very thoughtful about what I buy for myself. I never want to spend too much money on myself. But I have made a folder on my phone of pieces I regret not buying. We were in Paris and went to Alaïa and I wanted this pink set that I didn’t buy and I still think about it.”

“Now that we are approaching fall, I live in a long sleeve. That’s my Bay area [upbringing] coming out of me. I need to have a XXXL long-sleeve T-shirt, heavy cotton. That is my comfort layer. I bring one everywhere. That and a hoodie. Those are my two hometown pieces. And when I get obsessed with something, I find a lot of them. I have a lot of Supreme hoodies, although my new favorites are 6397, because they have fun colors.”

“I was thinking about what I would buy if I had all the money in the world. It’s a pair of cuffs—or, actually, anything from this jewelry designer Art Smith. It’s kind of modernist jewelry from a different era, like 50 years ago. So something like that. Sophie Buhai is kind of the modern-day equivalent. Pieces that could be in museums.”

“I’m going to be realistic—I did buy four pairs of shoes this week: Chanel flats [from Matthieu Blazy’s first collection] on the RealReal, a pair of [Michael Rider’s] Celine jazz shoes, two pairs of Bottega woven flats.”
